\title{\chg{Linearly implicit, energy-stable schemes of arbitrary temporal order

\begin{document}
\maketitle
\begin{abstract}
We develop linearly implicit, energy-stable schemes of arbitrary temporal order for a scalar-potential formulation of the Rosensweig ferrofluid model. By separating the weak formulation into symmetric dissipative, skew-symmetric bilinear, and skew-symmetric trilinear parts, we extrapolate only the first argument of the nonlinear form and obtain linear Runge--Kutta stage problems without losing the energy cancellation. For algebraically stable Runge--Kutta methods, we prove unique solvability for every positive time step, derive a discrete energy identity, and establish arbitrary-order temporal error estimates under explicit regularity, stage-order, quadrature, and starting-value assumptions. Compatible finite element spaces preserve the energy structure and recover the magnetic field as $\bs H_h=\nabla\varphi_h$, so that its curl vanishes exactly at every stage and time level. Numerical experiments illustrate the convergence and energy decay of the schemes and their application to three-dimensional magnetically driven channel flows. 
\end{abstract}
\noindent\textbf{Keywords:} \chg{Rosensweig ferrofluid model; linearly implicit Runge--Kutta method;
energy stability; high-order time.}

\section{Introduction}\label{sec:intro}


Ferrofluids are liquids containing suspended magnetic particles whose
motion can be controlled by an applied magnetic field. This property
makes them useful in sealing, damping, heat transfer, and magnetic
actuation \cite{Rosensweig1985,Rosensweig1987}. Two widely used
continuum models of ferrohydrodynamics are the Shliomis model
\cite{Shliomis1972Effective,Shliomis2002Ferrofluids} and the
Rosensweig model \cite{Rosensweig1985,Rosensweig1987}. Both describe
ferrofluids as homogeneous single-phase media, but they represent the
rotational dynamics of the magnetic particles differently. The
Rosensweig model introduces the angular velocity as an independent
unknown governed by an angular-momentum equation, whereas the Shliomis
model accounts for rotational effects through magnetic-torque terms
without introducing a separate angular-velocity equation. This
modeling distinction is important when comparing numerical methods
and their energy laws.

Because both models consist of strongly coupled nonlinear partial differential equations with nontrivial energy transfer, analytical solutions are rarely available, and numerical approximation is therefore the main practical approach. For the Shliomis model, Wu and Xie \cite{WuXie2023a} developed energy-stable mixed finite element semidiscretizations and fully discrete schemes, proved the existence and uniqueness of the discrete solutions, and derived optimal a priori error estimates. Mao and Sun \cite{MaoSun2025} analyzed a first-order energy-stable semi-implicit Euler finite element method for a simplified Shliomis system, including regularity results and unconditional error estimates. Zhang et al.  \cite{ZhangPan2025} proposed two linear and unconditionally energy-stable finite element schemes based on hybrid continuous--discontinuous and decoupled continuous spatial discretizations, respectively. Keram, Huang, and He \cite{KeramFlow2026} further studied a linear, decoupled, variable-step method and established its stability.

For the Rosensweig model, Nochetto, Salgado, and Tomas \cite{NochettoSalgadoTomas2016} gave a systematic study of the model and its numerical approximation. They constructed an energy-stable finite element scheme, proved the existence of discrete solutions, and established convergence under additional simplifying assumptions. Wu and Xie \cite{WuXie2025} developed energy-stable mixed finite element semidiscrete and fully discrete schemes, proved the existence and uniqueness of the discrete solutions, and obtained optimal error estimates based on regularity results. Dong et al.\ \cite{DongHuangHuangTang2025} proposed a semi-implicit, energy-stable fully discrete finite element method and proved its unconditional convergence to a weak solution. They later developed a second-order, linearized, and unconditionally energy-stable method whose energy law is written in terms of the original variables \cite{DongHuangHuangYang2026}. Zhang, He, and Yang \cite{ZhangHeYang2025} introduced a nonlocal auxiliary variable and a zero-energy-contribution decoupling strategy to obtain a fully decoupled, linear, second-order, and unconditionally energy-stable scheme. For a regularized Rosensweig system, Keram, Huang, and He \cite{KeramRosensweig2026} analyzed a linearized, decoupled, variable-step backward Euler method and proved unconditional stability and error estimates.

Beyond the FHD schemes reviewed above, high-order energy-stable
Runge--Kutta methods have been developed mainly for gradient-flow
problems. Shin et al.~\cite{ShinLeeLee2017} combined convex splitting
with additive Runge--Kutta methods to construct high-order,
unconditionally energy-stable schemes. Akrivis et
al.~\cite{HO_AkrivisLiLi2019} used extrapolation and the scalar
auxiliary variable approach to obtain linear Runge--Kutta schemes of
arbitrary order, while Shin and Lee~\cite{ShinLee2020} developed
high-order energy-stable Runge--Kutta methods for convex gradient
problems. Gong et al.~\cite{HO_GongZhaoWang2020} combined energy
quadratization with algebraically stable Runge--Kutta methods to
construct arbitrary-order linear schemes. Liu and
Yin~\cite{LiuYin2022} extended this approach to Runge--Kutta
discontinuous Galerkin schemes for fourth-order gradient flows, and
Tan and Tang~\cite{TanTang2022} studied a broader class of linear
energy-stable schemes based on scalar auxiliary variables and general
linear time discretizations. Sato et
al.~\cite{HO_SatoMiyatakeButcher2023} also developed high-order
linearly implicit schemes that preserve quadratic invariants. These
studies provide useful tools for high-order energy-stable time
integration. However, they do not directly cover the Rosensweig
system, where the pressure constraint, skew magnetic couplings,
curl--div spaces, and scalar-potential boundary conditions must be
treated together.


Motivated by the studies above, we develop high-order time
discretizations for the Rosensweig model that preserve its energy
structure and require only linear stage solves. Introducing the scalar
magnetic potential
\(
 \bs H=\nabla\varphi
\),
we write the Rosensweig FHD model in the abstract form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:intro-abstract}
\begin{aligned}
 \DD(\partial_t\bs x,\bs y)
 +\Aform(\bs x,\bs y)
 +\JJ(\bs x,\bs y)
 +\BB(\bs x,\bs x,\bs y)
 +\CC(\bs y,\widetilde p)
 &=
 \dual{\ff}{\bs y},
 \\
 \CC(\bs x,q)&=0.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here, \(\DD\) is the mass form, \(\Aform\) is symmetric and coercive,
\(\JJ\) is a skew-symmetric bilinear form, \(\BB\) is trilinear and
skew-symmetric in its last two arguments, and \(\CC\) represents the
incompressibility constraint. In particular,
\[
 \JJ(\bs y,\bs y)=0,
 \qquad
 \BB(\bs z,\bs y,\bs y)=0.
\]
These identities allow us to extrapolate the first argument of
\(\BB\) while treating the current stage state implicitly. Each
Runge--Kutta step is therefore linear, and the energy cancellation is
preserved.

Based on this structure, we construct linearly implicit Runge--Kutta
schemes of arbitrary temporal order. For algebraically stable
Runge--Kutta coefficients, we prove unique solvability for every
positive time step and derive an exact discrete energy identity.
Under the stage and quadrature conditions \(C(p-1)\) and \(B(p)\),
together with sufficient regularity and accurate starting values, we
establish order-\(p\) temporal error estimates directly in the
continuous function spaces. Backward Euler, extrapolated
Crank--Nicolson, and third- and fourth-order Gauss-type methods are
obtained as special cases. We also develop compatible finite element
schemes using MINI or Taylor--Hood elements for velocity and pressure,
and N\'ed\'elec and Raviart--Thomas elements for the magnetic
variables. The fully discrete schemes retain the energy estimate
without a restriction between the time step and the mesh size.
Moreover,
\[
 \bs H_h^n=\nabla\varphi_h^n,
 \qquad
 \bs H_{h,n,i}=\nabla\varphi_{h,n,i},
\]
so that
\[
 \curl\bs H_h^n=0,
 \qquad
 \curl\bs H_{h,n,i}=0
\]
at every time level and Runge--Kutta stage. This exact curl-free
property does not, by itself, enforce the magnetostatic divergence
relation.



The remainder of the paper is organized as follows.
Section~\ref{sec:model} introduces the Rosensweig model, function
spaces, and scalar-potential boundary closure.
Section~\ref{sec:abstract} defines the abstract variational forms and
their structural properties.
Section~\ref{sec:ho:arbitrary-order} develops the arbitrary-order
time discretization and presents the first- through fourth-order
examples.
Section~\ref{sec:FE} gives the compatible finite element schemes,
their implementation, and the block elimination.
Section~\ref{sec:numerics} presents the convergence, energy, and
three-dimensional flow experiments.






%Ferrofluids are liquids containing small magnetic particles. Mathematical models describe the interaction between fluid motion, particle rotation, magnetization, and the magnetic field. A numerical method must handle these interactions without introducing artificial growth of the total energy.

The magnetic force, magnetic torque, and transport terms exchange energy between the different components. Their cancellation is essential to the continuous energy law and should be retained by the discretization. Nochetto, Trivisa, and Weber \cite{NochettoTrivisaWeber2019} analyzed global weak solutions and convergence to equilibrium for the Rosensweig system. Wu and Xie \cite{WuXie2025} analyzed energy-stable mixed discretizations and a quasi-Newton solution procedure. Dong, Huang, Huang, and Yang \cite{DongHuangHuangYang2026} subsequently developed a fully discrete method with second-order temporal accuracy. \section{Model, spaces, and boundary compatibility}\label{sec:model}
\subsection{The Rosensweig equations}
Let $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^3$ be a bounded, simply connected, and convex domain with Lipschitz boundary $\partial\Omega$ and outward unit normal $\bs n$. Let $T>0$ be the final time. We consider an incompressible, viscous Newtonian ferrofluid occupying the domain $\Omega$ and subject to an externally applied magnetic field $\bs H_e$ satisfying the boundary condition $\bs H_e\cdot \bs n = 0$ on $\partial\Omega \times (0,T]$.  The unknowns are the velocity $\bs u$, pressure $p$, angular velocity $\bs\omega$, magnetization $\bs m$, and magnetic field $\bs H$. In $\Omega\times(0,T)$ they satisfy
\begin{subequations}\label{eq:FHD}
\begin{align}
\rho\bigl(\partial_t\bs u+(\bs u\cdot\nabla)\bs u\bigr)
 -(\eta+\zeta)\Delta\bs u+\nabla p
 &=\mu_0(\bs m\cdot\nabla)\bs H+2\zeta\curl\bs\omega,\label{eq:FHD-u}\\
\Div\bs u&=0,\label{eq:FHD-divu}\\
\rho\kappa\bigl(\partial_t\bs\omega+(\bs u\cdot\nabla)\bs\omega\bigr)
 -\eta'\Delta\bs\omega-(\eta'+\lambda')\nabla\Div\bs\omega
 &=\mu_0\bs m\times\bs H+2\zeta(\curl\bs u-2\bs\omega),\label{eq:FHD-w}\\
\partial_t\bs m+(\bs u\cdot\nabla)\bs m-\sigma\Delta\bs m
 &=\bs\omega\times\bs m-\tau^{-1}(\bs m-\chi_0\bs H),\label{eq:FHD-m}\\
\curl\bs H&=0,\label{eq:FHD-curlH}\\
\mu_0\Div(\bs H+\bs m)&=-\Div\bs H_e.\label{eq:FHD-divH}
\end{align}
\end{subequations}
All parameters $\rho,\kappa,\eta,\zeta,\eta',\lambda',\mu_0,\sigma,\tau,\chi_0$ are positive. In particular, $\sigma>0$ is needed for the coercivity used below. With the convention $\bs B=\mu_0(\bs H+\bs m)$, equation~\eqref{eq:FHD-divH} states that $\bs B+\bs H_e$, rather than $\bs B$ alone, is divergence-free.
%
We prescribe initial values 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:initial-FHD}
\bs u(0) = \bs u_0,\qquad\bs\omega(0) = \bs\omega_0,\qquad\bs m(0) = \bs m_0,
\end{equation} 
with $\Div\bs u_0=0$, and boundary conditions
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bc}
 \bs u=\bs\omega=0,\qquad \bs m\cdot\bs n=0,\qquad
 (\curl\bs m)\times\bs n=0,\qquad \bs H\times\bs n=0
 \quad\text{on }\partial\Omega.
\end{equation}
The condition on $\curl\bs m$ is the natural boundary condition for the curl--div realization of the magnetization diffusion. 
%It cannot be omitted when this realization is identified with a strong vector Laplacian. We retain $\bs H_e\cdot\bs n=0$ when such a trace is prescribed for the data, but this condition is not a substitute for the boundary compatibility discussed below.

\subsection{Function spaces and norms}
We write $(\cdot,\cdot)$ for the scalar or vector $L^2(\Omega)$ inner product. Standard Sobolev norms are denoted by $\|\cdot\|_{H^s}$; a norm without a subscript is the $L^2$ norm. Set
\begin{gather*}
 \bs S=H_0^1(\Omega)^3,\qquad \Sigma=H_0^1(\Omega),\qquad W=L_0^2(\Omega),\\
 \bs U=H_0(\curl;\Omega),\qquad\bs V=H_0(\Div;\Omega),\\
 D(-\Delta_D)=\{\psi\in\Sigma:\Delta\psi\in L^2(\Omega)\},\\
 \V=\bs S\times\bs S\times(\bs V\cap H(\curl;\Omega))\times D(-\Delta_D).
\end{gather*}
Here $H_0(\curl)$ imposes a zero tangential trace, $H_0(\Div)$ imposes a zero normal trace, and $L_0^2$ imposes zero mean. 
%Normal traces are imposed on $H(\Div)$ fields, not on arbitrary $L^2$ fields.

For $\bs y=(\bs v,\bs s,\bs F,\psi)\in\V$, let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Vnorm}
 \|\bs y\|_{\V}^2=
 \|\nabla\bs v\|^2+\|\nabla\bs s\|^2+\|\bs F\|^2
 +\|\Div\bs F\|^2+\|\curl\bs F\|^2
 +\|\nabla\psi\|^2+\|\Delta\psi\|^2.
\end{equation}
We assume that 
$\Omega$ is sufficiently smooth so that, for all $\bs F\in \bigl(H_0(\curl)\cap H(\Div)\bigr)\cup\bigl(H(\curl)\cap H_0(\Div)\bigr)$ and $\psi \in H_0^1(\Omega) \cap H^2(\Omega)$, the following spatial regularity estimates hold: 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:domain-reg}
 \|\bs F\|_{H^1}\le C\bigl(\|\curl\bs F\|+\|\Div\bs F\|\bigr),
 \qquad
 \|\psi\|_{H^2}\le C\|\Delta\psi\|.
\end{equation}
%They are understood for the spaces just defined. We explicitly require \eqref{eq:domain-reg}; they must not be assumed for an arbitrary Lipschitz domain. The curl--div setting and compatible discretizations are discussed in \cite{Hiptmair2002,ArnoldFalkWinther2006,ArnoldFalkWinther2010}. Throughout the analysis, $C$ may depend on the domain, physical parameters, final time, and stated exact-solution bounds, but not on $\dt$. Constants in finite-dimensional norm equivalences may depend on the mesh and are marked with a subscript $h$.

\subsection{Scalar potential and the additional boundary closure}\label{subsec:boundary}
\cref{eq:FHD-curlH}, together with the boundary condition $\bs H\times\bs n=0$ on $\partial\Omega\times (0,T]$ and \cref{eq:FHD-divH}, implies there exists a unique scalar magnetic potential $\varphi \in H_0^1(\Omega)$ such that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:potential}
 \bs H=\nabla\varphi,\qquad
 \mu_0\Delta\varphi+\mu_0\Div\bs m=-\Div\bs H_e.
\end{equation}
The initial value of the potential $\varphi$ is fixed by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:phi0}
 -\mu_0\Delta\varphi_0=\mu_0\Div\bs m_0+\Div\bs H_e(0),
 \qquad\varphi_0|_{\partial\Omega}=0.
\end{equation}
Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:abeta}
 \alpha =\frac{\mu_0(1+\chi_0)+\chi_0}{\tau},\qquad
 \beta=\frac{\mu_0+\chi_0}{\mu_0\tau}.
\end{equation}
Differentiating the last equation of \eqref{eq:potential}, using \eqref{eq:FHD-m}, and adding a multiple of the same constraint gives the bulk equation
\begin{equation}\label{eq:potential-evolution}
\begin{aligned}
 &-\mu_0\Delta\partial_t\varphi-\mu_0\Div(\bs\omega\times\bs m)
 -\frac{\chi_0}{\tau}\Div\bs m-\alpha\Delta\varphi\\
 &\qquad+\mu_0\Div\bigl((\bs u\cdot\nabla)\bs m\bigr)+\mu_0\sigma\Delta^2\varphi
 =\Div\partial_t\bs H_e+\beta\Div\bs H_e-\sigma\Delta\Div\bs H_e.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation} 
\cref{eq:potential-evolution} is fourth order in space with respect to $\varphi$, the existing boundary condition $\varphi = 0$ on $\partial\Omega$ alone is insufficient to specify the boundary value problem. We therefore impose an artificial boundary condition on $\Delta\varphi$, i.e., we consider the Navier boundary condition for $\varphi$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:navier}
 \varphi=0,\qquad \Delta\varphi=0\quad\text{on }\partial\Omega.
\end{equation}
%For smooth $\varphi$ and $\psi$ with $\psi|_{\partial\Omega}=0$, Green's formula gives
%\begin{equation}\label{eq:green-biharmonic}
% (\Delta^2\varphi,\psi)
% = (\Delta\varphi,\Delta\psi)
% -\int_{\partial\Omega}\Delta\varphi\,\partial_{\bs n}\psi\dd S.
%\end{equation}
%Consequently, replacing the fourth-order term by $(\Delta\varphi,\Delta\psi)$ without a boundary load introduces the Navier condition
%\begin{equation}\label{eq:navier}
% \varphi=0,\qquad \Delta\varphi=0\quad\text{on }\partial\Omega.
%\end{equation}
%The mixed representation $\vartheta=-\Delta\varphi$ with $\vartheta\in H_0^1(\Omega)$ imposes the same condition. The second condition in \eqref{eq:navier} does not follow from \eqref{eq:bc}. In this paper, the abstract evolution problem is defined with this Dirichlet--Navier closure. At energy regularity the closure is encoded variationally; \eqref{eq:navier} is its interpretation for smooth solutions.


\section{An abstract formulation}\label{sec:abstract}
\subsection{The dissipative and skew-symmetric forms}
%Subtracting the mean fixes the pressure gauge and does not change its gradient. 
%The product variables in \eqref{eq:auxiliary} are ordinary products wherever the needed regularity holds. No $L^2$ projection onto $H_0^1$ or $H_0(\curl)$ is used: these spaces are not closed subspaces of $L^2$, so such orthogonal projections are not defined on all of $L^2$.

Let
\[
 \bs x=(\bs u,\bs\omega,\bs m,\varphi),\quad
 \bs y=(\bs v,\bs s,\bs F,\psi),\quad
 \bs z=(\bs\epsilon,\bs \xi,\bs A,\phi).
\]
We use the scalar transport forms
\begin{align}
 b(\bs\epsilon,\bs u,\bs v)
 &=\frac12\bigl(((\bs\epsilon\cdot\nabla)\bs u,\bs v)
                  -((\bs\epsilon\cdot\nabla)\bs v,\bs u)\bigr),\label{eq:b}\\
 c(\bs\epsilon,\bs m,\bs F)
 &=\frac12\bigl((\bs\epsilon\cdot\bs m,\Div\bs F)
                  -(\bs\epsilon\cdot\bs F,\Div\bs m)\bigr).\label{eq:c}
\end{align}
The mass and dissipative forms are
%
\begin{equation}\label{eq:D}
 \DD(\bs x,\bs y)=\rho(\bs u,\bs v)+\rho\kappa(\bs\omega,\bs s)
 +(\bs m,\bs F)+\mu_0(\nabla\varphi,\nabla\psi)
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:A}
\begin{aligned}
 \Aform(\bs x,\bs y)={}&\eta(\nabla\bs u,\nabla\bs v)
 +\eta'(\nabla\bs\omega,\nabla\bs s)
 +(\eta'+\lambda')(\Div\bs\omega,\Div\bs s)\\
 &+\sigma(\curl\bs m,\curl\bs F)+\sigma(\Div\bs m,\Div\bs F)
 +\tau^{-1}(\bs m,\bs F)\\
 &+\alpha(\nabla\varphi,\nabla\psi)+\sigma\mu_0(\Delta\varphi,\Delta\psi)
 +\zeta(\curl\bs u-2\bs\omega,\curl\bs v-2\bs s).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
We separate the linear skew coupling and the pressure constraint:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:J-C}
 \JJ(\bs x,\bs y)=\frac{\chi_0}{\tau}\bigl((\bs m,\nabla\psi)-(\bs F,\nabla\varphi)\bigr),
 \qquad \CC(\bs y,q)=-(\Div\bs v,q).
\end{equation}
The nonlinear form is genuinely trilinear:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:B}
\begin{aligned}
 \BB(\bs z,\bs x,\bs y)={}&\rho b(\bs\epsilon,\bs u,\bs v)
 +\rho\kappa b(\bs\epsilon,\bs\omega,\bs s)-c(\bs\epsilon,\bs m,\bs F)-\tfrac12(\bs m\times\curl\bs\epsilon,\bs F)-(\bs \xi\times\bs m,\bs F)\\
 &+\tfrac{\mu_0}{2}\bigl\{(\nabla\varphi,\bs v\,\Div\bs A)
                         -(\nabla\psi,\bs u\,\Div\bs A) + (\bs u\times\curl\bs A,\nabla\psi)
                         -(\bs v\times\curl\bs A,\nabla\varphi)\bigr\}\\
 &+\tfrac{\mu_0}{2}\bigl[(\bs A\times\curl\bs u,\nabla\psi)
                         -(\bs A\times\curl\bs v,\nabla\varphi)\bigr] +\mu_0\bigl[(\bs\omega\times\bs A,\nabla\psi)
                    -(\bs s\times\bs A,\nabla\varphi)\bigr]\\
 &+\tfrac12\bigl[(\bs\epsilon\times\bs F,\curl\bs m)
                      -(\bs\epsilon\times\bs m,\curl\bs F)\bigr]+\tfrac{\mu_0}{2}\bigl[(\Delta\psi,\bs u\cdot\bs A)
                         -(\Delta\varphi,\bs v\cdot\bs A)\bigr].
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:forms}
Under \eqref{eq:domain-reg}, the forms are well defined on $\V$. The form $\Aform$ is bounded, symmetric, and coercive. The form $\JJ$ is bounded and skew-symmetric, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:skew}
 \BB(\bs z,\bs x,\bs y)=-\BB(\bs z,\bs y,\bs x),\qquad
 |\BB(\bs z,\bs x,\bs y)|\le C\|\bs z\|_{\V}\|\bs x\|_{\V}\|\bs y\|_{\V}.
\end{equation}
The pressure form is bounded and satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:infsup}
 \sup_{\bs y\in\V\setminus\{0\}}\frac{|\CC(\bs y,q)|}{\|\bs y\|_{\V}}
 \ge c\|q\|,\qquad q\in W.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Completing a square gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:A-square}
\begin{aligned}
 \Aform(\bs y,\bs y)={}&\eta\|\nabla\bs v\|^2+\eta'\|\nabla\bs s\|^2
 +(\eta'+\lambda')\|\Div\bs s\|^2+\zeta\|\curl\bs v-2\bs s\|^2\\
 &+\sigma\bigl(\|\curl\bs F\|^2+\|\Div\bs F\|^2\bigr)+\tau^{-1}\|\bs F\|^2
 +\alpha\|\nabla\psi\|^2+\sigma\mu_0\|\Delta\psi\|^2.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Every coefficient needed in \eqref{eq:Vnorm} is positive. Thus
$c\|\bs y\|_{\V}^2\le\Aform(\bs y,\bs y)\le C\|\bs y\|_{\V}^2$.
Symmetry and boundedness follow directly. The two terms of $\JJ$ change sign when the arguments are exchanged. Each bracket in \eqref{eq:B} has the same property. The remaining cross-product terms are also skew because $(\bs a\times\bs b,\bs c)=-(\bs a\times\bs c,\bs b)$.

For continuity, \eqref{eq:domain-reg} places the magnetic vector fields and the potential gradients in $H^1$. Use $H^1(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^6(\Omega)$, interpolation into $L^3$ or $L^4$, and H\"older's inequality. For example,
\[
 |(\nabla\varphi,\bs v\,\Div\bs A)|
 \le\|\nabla\varphi\|_{L^3}\|\bs v\|_{L^6}\|\Div\bs A\|,
 \qquad
 |(\Delta\psi,\bs u\cdot\bs A)|
 \le\|\Delta\psi\|\|\bs u\|_{L^4}\|\bs A\|_{L^4}.
\]
The other terms are estimated in the same way. Finally, for each $q\in L_0^2$ choose $\bs v\in H_0^1(\Omega)^3$ with $\Div\bs v=q$ and $\|\nabla\bs v\|\le C\|q\|$. Testing the supremum with $(\bs v,0,0,0)$ gives \eqref{eq:infsup}.
\end{proof}


%Set
%\begin{equation}\label{eq:load}
% \bs g_e=\partial_t\bs H_e+\beta\bs H_e-\sigma\nabla\Div\bs H_e,
% \qquad \dual{\ff}{\bs y}=-(\bs g_e,\nabla\psi).
%\end{equation}
%This fixes the sign of the external-field diffusion term in both the continuous and discrete formulations. More general body forces can be included as described in \cref{subsec:manufactured}.






%\subsection{Structural properties}


Write $\|\bs y\|_{\Aform}=\Aform(\bs y,\bs y)^{1/2}$ and $\|\bs y\|_{\DD}=\DD(\bs y,\bs y)^{1/2}$. Let $\mathbb H_D$ be the completion of $\V$ in the $\DD$ norm. For a linear functional $\ell$, define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dualnorms}
 \|\ell\|_{\Aform^*}=\sup_{\bs y\ne0}\frac{|\dual{\ell}{\bs y}|}{\|\bs y\|_{\Aform}},
 \qquad
 \|\ell\|_{\DD^*}=\sup_{\bs y\ne0}\frac{|\dual{\ell}{\bs y}|}{\|\bs y\|_{\DD}}.
\end{equation}
The second quantity need not be finite for an arbitrary member of $\V'$.
% For \eqref{eq:load} it satisfies
%$\|\ff\|_{\DD^*}\le\mu_0^{-1/2}\|\bs g_e\|$.
%Thus $\bs g_e\in L^1(0,T;L^2(\Omega)^3)$ is sufficient for the $L^1$ forcing estimates below. In particular, control of $\Div\bs H_e$ alone is not enough to justify that bound.

\subsection{Evolution problem and continuous energy balance}

For smooth vector fields, the identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:vector-identity}
\begin{aligned}
 (\bs u\cdot\nabla)\bs m={}&\frac12\bs u\,\Div\bs m-\frac12\bs m\,\Div\bs u
 +\frac12\nabla(\bs u\cdot\bs m)\\
 &+\frac12\curl\bs u\times\bs m+\frac12\curl\bs m\times\bs u
 +\frac12\curl(\bs m\times\bs u)
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
rewrites magnetization transport in a form suitable for curl--div spaces. The same identity, with $(\bs u,\bs m)$ replaced by $(\bs m,\bs H)$, rewrites the magnetic force. Introduce
%\begin{equation}\label{eq:auxiliary}
% \bs z=\bs u\times\bs m,\qquad \bs k=\curl\bs m,\qquad
% \vartheta=-\Delta\varphi,\qquad \varpi=\bs u\cdot\bs m.
%\end{equation}
the shifted pressure 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:shifted-pressure}
 \pp=p-\frac{\mu_0}{2}\bs m\cdot\nabla\varphi
 -\frac1{|\Omega|}\int_\Omega\left(p-\frac{\mu_0}{2}\bs m\cdot\nabla\varphi\right)\dd x.
\end{equation}
The weak formulation of \eqref{eq:FHD}, with the magnetostatic relation replaced by \eqref{eq:potential-evolution}, reads as follows:
find $\bs x(t)\in\V$ and $\pp(t)\in W$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:abstract}
\begin{aligned}
 \DD(\partial_t\bs x,\bs y)+\Aform(\bs x,\bs y)+\JJ(\bs x,\bs y)
 +\BB(\bs x,\bs x,\bs y)+\CC(\bs y,\pp)&=\dual{\ff}{\bs y},&&\bs y\in\V,\\
 \CC(\bs x,q)&=0,&&q\in W,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
with $\bs x(0)=(\bs u_0,\bs\omega_0,\bs m_0,\varphi_0)$ and $\ff = \Div\partial_t\bs H_e + \beta \Div\bs H_e - \sigma\Delta \Div\bs H_e$. 

%There is a separate issue when the algebraic magnetostatic relation is replaced by an evolution equation \eqref{eq:potential-evolution}. Define its residual by
%\begin{equation}\label{eq:residual-r}
% r=\mu_0\Delta\varphi+\mu_0\Div\bs m+\Div\bs H_e.
%\end{equation}
%Taking the divergence of \eqref{eq:FHD-m} and combining it with \eqref{eq:potential-evolution} yields
%\begin{equation}\label{eq:r-evolution}
% \partial_t r-\sigma\Delta r+\beta r=0,\qquad \text{in }\Omega,
%\end{equation}
%with initial condition $r(0) = 0$. This alone does not imply $r=0$ on a bounded domain without suitable boundary data for \eqref{eq:r-evolution}.
%
%\begin{proposition}[Conditional relation to the original model]\label{prop:equivalence}
%A sufficiently smooth solution of \eqref{eq:FHD}--\eqref{eq:bc} that also satisfies \eqref{eq:navier} satisfies the abstract formulation in \eqref{eq:abstract}. Conversely, a sufficiently smooth solution of that abstract formulation satisfies the original bulk equations provided $r(0)=0$ and the residual equation \eqref{eq:r-evolution} has homogeneous boundary data, for example $r|_{\partial\Omega}=0$ or $\partial_{\bs n}r|_{\partial\Omega}=0$.
%\end{proposition}
%\begin{proof}
%For the forward direction, use \eqref{eq:potential}, the vector identity \eqref{eq:vector-identity} below, and integration by parts with \eqref{eq:bc} and \eqref{eq:navier}. This gives the forms \eqref{eq:abstract}. For the reverse direction, the component equations give \eqref{eq:FHD-u}--\eqref{eq:FHD-m} and \eqref{eq:potential-evolution}. Testing \eqref{eq:r-evolution} with $r$, with either of the stated homogeneous residual boundary conditions, gives
%\[
% \frac12\frac{\dd}{\dd t}\|r\|^2+\sigma\|\nabla r\|^2+\beta\|r\|^2=0.
%\]
%Since $r(0)=0$, the residual vanishes. Equation~\eqref{eq:FHD-divH} follows, and $\curl\nabla\varphi=0$ gives \eqref{eq:FHD-curlH}.
%\end{proof}
%
%\begin{remark}[What the equivalence statement does not assert]\label{rem:boundary-scope}
%The additional trace conditions in \cref{prop:equivalence} are compatibility hypotheses, not consequences of the boundary conditions originally stated for the Rosensweig model. They may restrict the admissible data. We do not prove that a general solution of the abstract evolution problem satisfies them. In particular, the regularity results for a formulation with $\bs H\cdot\bs n=0$, such as the realization used in \cite{WuXie2025}, cannot simply be transferred to the present condition $\bs H\times\bs n=0$. All subsequent error estimates assume the required regularity of the exact solution rather than claiming a new regularity theorem for the full three-dimensional system.
%\end{remark}
%
%
%The relation of this problem to the physical boundary-value problem is exactly the conditional one in \cref{prop:equivalence}. For the temporal analysis, an exact solution of \eqref{eq:abstract} is assumed to exist with the stated regularity.

\begin{theorem}[Continuous energy estimate]\label{thm:continuous-energy}
Suppose that $\bs x$ is regular enough to be used as a test function and to satisfy the chain rule for the $\DD$ norm, and that $\ff\in L^1(0,T;\mathbb H_D')$. Then, for $0\le t\le T$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:energy-cont-identity}
 \|\bs x(t)\|_{\DD}^2+2\int_0^t\|\bs x(s)\|_{\Aform}^2\dd s
 =\|\bs x(0)\|_{\DD}^2+2\int_0^t\dual{\ff(s)}{\bs x(s)}\dd s.
\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:energy-cont-bound}
 \sup_{0\le t\le T}\left[\|\bs x(t)\|_{\DD}^2+
 2\int_0^t\|\bs x(s)\|_{\Aform}^2\dd s\right]^{1/2}
 \le\|\bs x(0)\|_{\DD}+2\int_0^T\|\ff(s)\|_{\DD^*}\dd s.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Set $\bs y=\bs x$ and $q=\pp$ in \eqref{eq:abstract}. Both skew terms and the pressure term vanish. Therefore
\[
 \tfrac12\tfrac{\dd}{\dd t}\|\bs x\|_{\DD}^2+\|\bs x\|_{\Aform}^2=\dual{\ff}{\bs x}.
\]
Integration gives \eqref{eq:energy-cont-identity}. If $M$ denotes the supremum on the left of \eqref{eq:energy-cont-bound} and $F=\int_0^T\|\ff\|_{\DD^*}\dd s$, then $\sup_t\|\bs x(t)\|_{\DD}\le M$ and $M^2\le\|\bs x(0)\|_{\DD}^2+2FM$. The elementary quadratic inequality gives $M\le\|\bs x(0)\|_{\DD}+2F$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:max-energy}
The supremum in \eqref{eq:energy-cont-bound} is taken over the entire cumulative expression. It cannot be replaced, with the same right-hand side, by $\sup_t\|\bs x(t)\|_{\DD}^2+2\int_0^T\|\bs x\|_{\Aform}^2$. With zero forcing, the first term already contains the initial energy, so adding all later dissipation would generally exceed it. Also, zero forcing implies energy dissipation, not constancy of the state norm.
\end{remark}

\subsection{A nonlinear estimate used in the error analysis}
To state sufficient regularity explicitly, set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:regularity-norm}
 \|\bs x\|_{\mathcal R}=
 \|\bs u\|_{W^{1,\infty}}+\|\bs\omega\|_{W^{1,\infty}}
 +\|\bs m\|_{W^{1,\infty}}+\|\varphi\|_{W^{2,\infty}},
 \qquad \mathcal R=\{\bs x\in\V:\|\bs x\|_{\mathcal R}<\infty\}.
\end{equation}
This is a sufficient smoothness class, not a claimed minimal one.
\begin{lemma}[Estimate with one smooth argument]\label{lem:smooth-factor}
For $\bs x\in\mathcal R$ and $\bs z,\bs y\in\V$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:smooth-factor}
 |\BB(\bs z,\bs x,\bs y)|\le C\|\bs x\|_{\mathcal R}\|\bs z\|_{\DD}\|\bs y\|_{\Aform}.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Derivatives on the first argument must be transferred before the estimate is applied. For example, the zero normal trace of $\bs A$ gives
\[
 (\nabla\varphi,\bs v\,\Div\bs A)
 =-(\bs A,\nabla(\bs v\cdot\nabla\varphi)),
 \qquad
 (\nabla\psi,\bs u\,\Div\bs A)
 =-(\bs A,\nabla(\bs u\cdot\nabla\psi)).
\]
Both right-hand sides are bounded by
$C\|\bs x\|_{\mathcal R}\|\bs A\|\|\bs y\|_{\Aform}$, using \eqref{eq:domain-reg}. Similarly,
\[
 (\bs u\times\curl\bs A,\nabla\psi)
 =(\bs A,\curl(\nabla\psi\times\bs u)),
\]
where the boundary term is zero because $\bs u=0$. The analogous term containing $\bs v$ is treated in the same way. In $(\bs m\times\curl\bs\epsilon,\bs F)$, transfer the curl to $\bs F\times\bs m$; $\bs\epsilon=0$ removes the boundary term. Then \eqref{eq:domain-reg} controls $\bs F$ in $H^1$.

The terms without derivatives on $\bs z$ use only its $L^2$ components. For instance,
\[
 |(\Delta\psi,\bs u\cdot\bs A)|
 \le\|\bs u\|_{L^\infty}\|\bs A\|\|\Delta\psi\|,
 \quad
 |(\Delta\varphi,\bs v\cdot\bs A)|
 \le\|\Delta\varphi\|_{L^\infty}\|\bs A\|\|\bs v\|.
\]
The transport and remaining cross-product terms are bounded by the same argument. Summing these estimates proves \eqref{eq:smooth-factor}.
\end{proof}

%\begin{currentchange}
\section{Arbitrary-order linearly implicit time discretization}
\label{sec:ho:arbitrary-order}

This section discretizes time only. All states remain in the continuous space
$\V$, and all pressures remain in $W$. We use the forms in
\cref{sec:abstract}; no mesh, finite element space, or discrete spatial operator
is introduced here. The boundary closure and the compatibility conditions in
\cref{subsec:boundary} remain in force.

We combine extrapolation with algebraically stable Runge--Kutta methods.
Related high-order constructions for gradient flows are studied in
\cite{HO_AkrivisLiLi2019,HO_GongZhaoWang2020}. The present energy is already a
quadratic form, so no additional energy variable is needed. We first prove
solvability and an energy identity in the continuous spaces. We then give
explicit stage conditions that imply arbitrary-order temporal convergence.
The number of stages is chosen to meet these conditions, rather than inferred
from the classical order of an ordinary differential equation method alone.

\subsection{The time-semidiscrete scheme}
Let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:kernel}
 \mathfrak K=\{\bs y\in\V:\CC(\bs y,q)=0\text{ for all }q\in W\},
 \qquad E(\bs x)=\tfrac12\DD(\bs x,\bs x).
\end{equation}
The kernel is a closed subspace of $\V$. Recall that $\Aform$ is symmetric and
coercive, whereas $\JJ$ and the last two arguments of $\BB$ are skew-symmetric.
In particular, $\JJ(\bs y,\bs y)=\BB(\bs z,\bs y,\bs y)=0$ for all $\bs y,\bs z\in\V$.

Fix an extrapolation order $p\ge1$. Let
$\mathsf A=(a_{ij})\in\mathbb R^{s\times s}$,
$\boldsymbol b=(b_1,\ldots,b_s)^{\mathsf T}$, and
$\boldsymbol c=(c_1,\ldots,c_s)^{\mathsf T}$
be the coefficients of an $s$-stage Runge--Kutta method.
They are written in the Butcher tableau
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:butcher-tableau}
\begin{array}{c|cccc}
c_1    & a_{11} & a_{12} & \cdots & a_{1s} \\
c_2    & a_{21} & a_{22} & \cdots & a_{2s} \\
\vdots & \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots \\
c_s    & a_{s1} & a_{s2} & \cdots & a_{ss} \\
\hline
       & b_1    & b_2    & \cdots & b_s
\end{array}.
\end{equation}
Here $c_i$ specifies the stage time $t_n+c_i k$,
$a_{ij}$ determines the contribution of the $j$th stage
derivative to the $i$th stage state, and $b_i$ is the
weight used in the endpoint update.
The stage number $s$ is chosen separately from the
extrapolation order $p$.

For solvability
and energy stability we require only
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:rk-assumptions}
 \mathsf A\text{ invertible},\qquad b_i>0,\qquad
 0\le c_i\le1,\qquad \bs c=\mathsf A\bs1,\qquad \mathsf G\succeq0,
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:G}
 \mathsf G=(g_{ij}),\qquad
 g_{ij}=b_i a_{ij}+b_j a_{ji}-b_i b_j.
\end{equation}
The last condition, together with nonnegative weights, is algebraic stability
\cite{HO_HairerWanner1996}. Gauss and Radau IIA methods meet
\eqref{eq:ho:rk-assumptions}; their classical orders are $2s$ and $2s-1$,
respectively. Gauss methods have $\mathsf G=0$. Accuracy requirements beyond
\eqref{eq:ho:rk-assumptions} will be stated separately.

Write $\dt=T/N$, $t_n=n\dt$, and assume $N\ge p$. Given
$\bs x^0,\ldots,\bs x^{p-1}\in\mathfrak K$, set, for $n=p-1,\ldots,N-1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:predictor}
 \widehat{\bs x}_{n,i}=\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}\ell_j(c_i)\bs x^{n-j},\qquad
 \ell_j(c)=\prod_{\substack{0\le m\le p-1\\m\ne j}}
                    \frac{c+m}{m-j},\qquad t_{n,i}=t_n+c_i \dt.
\end{equation}
For $p=1$ the empty product equals one. Find stage states
$\bs x_{n,i}\in\V$, stage derivatives $\mathcal K_{n,i}\in\V$, and pressures
$P_{n,i}\in W$ such that
\begin{subequations}\label{eq:ho:scheme}
\begin{align}
 \bs x_{n,i}&=\bs x^n+\dt\sum_{j=1}^s a_{ij}\mathcal K_{n,j},
                                      \label{eq:ho:stage}\\
 \DD(\mathcal K_{n,i},\bs y)+\Aform(\bs x_{n,i},\bs y)
 &+\JJ(\bs x_{n,i},\bs y)+\BB(\widehat{\bs x}_{n,i},\bs x_{n,i},\bs y)+\CC(\bs y,P_{n,i})=\dual{\ff(t_{n,i})}{\bs y},
                                      \label{eq:ho:stage-equation}\\
 \CC(\bs x_{n,i},q)&=0,\qquad i=1,\ldots,s,
                                      \label{eq:ho:stage-constraint}\\
 \bs x^{n+1}&=\bs x^n+\dt\sum_{i=1}^s b_i\mathcal K_{n,i}.
                                      \label{eq:ho:update}
\end{align}
\end{subequations}
The equations hold for all $\bs y\in\V$ and $q\in W$. The first argument of
$\BB$ is known, while its second argument is an unknown stage state.
Consequently, each step is a coupled linear variational problem. The pressures
are independent stage multipliers, not averages of endpoint pressures.

\subsection{Solvability and the energy identity}
\begin{theorem}[Unique solvability in the continuous spaces]
\label{thm:ho:solvability}
Assume \cref{lem:forms} and \eqref{eq:ho:rk-assumptions}. Given
$\bs x^n\in\mathfrak K$, predictors $\widehat{\bs x}_{n,i}\in\V$, and
$\ff(t_{n,i})\in\V'$, problem~\eqref{eq:ho:scheme} has a unique solution for
 every $k>0$. Its stage derivatives and updated state belong to $\mathfrak K$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Put $\mathsf S=\mathsf A^{-1}$ and
$\mathsf W_b=\operatorname{diag}(b_1,\ldots,b_s)$. Eliminating the stage
derivatives gives, on $\mathfrak K^s = \mathfrak K \times \cdots\times \mathfrak K$, \cref{eq:ho:scheme} becomes: Find $\mathbf U = (\bs x_{n,1},\dots,\bs x_{n,s})^\intercal \in \mathfrak K^s$, such that
$$
a_n(\mathbf U,\mathbf V) = L_n(\mathbf V),\qquad\forall~\mathbf V \in \mathfrak K^s,
$$
with the bilinear form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:product-form}
\begin{aligned}
 a_n(\mathbf U,\mathbf V)={}&
 \frac1\dt\sum_{i,j=1}^s b_i S_{ij}\DD(U_j,V_i)+\sum_{i=1}^s b_i\bigl[
   \Aform(U_i,V_i)+\JJ(U_i,V_i)
       +\BB(\widehat{\bs x}_{n,i},U_i,V_i)\bigr],\quad U_j = \bs x_{n,j}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and the right-hand side 
\[
L_n(\mathbf V) =  \sum_{i=1}^s b_i\dual{\ff(t_{n,i})}{V_i}
 +\frac1\dt\sum_{i=1}^s b_i(\mathsf S\bs1)_i\DD(\bs x^n,V_i).
\]
Both the form and the load are bounded on $\mathfrak K^s$ for the prescribed
predictors. The identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:inverse-positivity}
 \frac{\mathsf W_b\mathsf S+\mathsf S^{\mathsf T}\mathsf W_b}{2}
 =\frac12\mathsf A^{-\mathsf T}
            (\mathsf G+\bs b\bs b^{\mathsf T})\mathsf A^{-1}
 \succeq0
\end{equation}
shows that the mass term in $a_n(\mathbf U,\mathbf U)$ is nonnegative.
The skew terms vanish, so
\[
 a_n(\mathbf U,\mathbf U)\ge
 \sum_{i=1}^s b_i\|U_i\|_{\Aform}^2
 \ge c\min_i b_i\sum_{i=1}^s\|U_i\|_{\V}^2.
\]
Lax--Milgram therefore gives unique stages in $\mathfrak K^s$.
Set
\[
 \mathcal K_{n,i}=\dt^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^s S_{ij}(\bs x_{n,j}-\bs x^n)\in\mathfrak K.
\]
For each stage the remaining residual is a bounded functional on $\V$ that
vanishes on $\mathfrak K$. The continuous inf-sup condition in
\eqref{eq:infsup} yields a unique pressure $P_{n,i}\in W$.
Finally, \eqref{eq:ho:update} preserves membership in $\mathfrak K$.
%This is an infinite-dimensional existence argument; uniqueness alone would
%not suffice to establish existence.
\end{proof}

%{\color{red}{stop here!!!}}

\begin{theorem}[Unconditional energy stability]\label{thm:ho:energy-solvability}
Under the hypotheses of \cref{thm:ho:solvability},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:energy-identity}
\begin{aligned}
 &E(\bs x^{n+1})-E(\bs x^n)+\dt\sum_{i=1}^s b_i\|\bs x_{n,i}\|_{\Aform}^2\\
 &\qquad+\frac{\dt^2}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^sg_{ij}
               \DD(\mathcal K_{n,i},\mathcal K_{n,j})
 =\dt\sum_{i=1}^s b_i\dual{\ff(t_{n,i})}{\bs x_{n,i}}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:forced-bound}
 E(\bs x^{n+1})+\frac{\dt}{2}\sum_{i=1}^s b_i\|\bs x_{n,i}\|_{\Aform}^2
 \le E(\bs x^n)+\frac{\dt}{2}\sum_{i=1}^s b_i\|\ff(t_{n,i})\|_{\Aform^*}^2.
\end{equation}
If $\ff=0$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:unforced-bound}
 E(\bs x^{n+1})+\dt\sum_{i=1}^s b_i\|\bs x_{n,i}\|_{\Aform}^2\le E(\bs x^n).
\end{equation}
For Gauss methods, i.e., $\mathsf G=0$, \eqref{eq:ho:unforced-bound} is an equality.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Test \eqref{eq:ho:stage-equation} with $\bs x_{n,i}$. The skew terms and pressure
term vanish, giving
\[
 \DD(\mathcal K_{n,i},\bs x_{n,i})+\|\bs x_{n,i}\|_{\Aform}^2
 =\dual{\ff(t_{n,i})}{\bs x_{n,i}}.
\]
Expanding the quadratic energy of the update and using the stage relations yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:quadratic-identity}
 E(\bs x^{n+1})-E(\bs x^n)
 =\dt\sum_{i=1}^s b_i\DD(\mathcal K_{n,i},\bs x_{n,i})
 -\frac{\dt^2}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^sg_{ij}\DD(\mathcal K_{n,i},\mathcal K_{n,j}).
\end{equation}
This proves \eqref{eq:ho:energy-identity}. The last sum is nonnegative:
if $\mathsf G=\mathsf R^{\mathsf T}\mathsf R$, it equals
$\sum_r\|\sum_iR_{ri}\mathcal K_{n,i}\|_{\DD}^2$.
Young's inequality gives \eqref{eq:ho:forced-bound}, and zero forcing gives
\eqref{eq:ho:unforced-bound}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:ho:stability-scope}
Energy stability holds for every prescribed predictor in $\V$, regardless of
its accuracy or the signs of its extrapolation coefficients. It does not imply
arbitrary-order convergence by itself. Also, $\mathsf G=0$ removes the additional
Runge--Kutta dissipation, not the physical dissipation in $\Aform$.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Arbitrary-order temporal error estimates}
We use sufficient stage and quadrature conditions that can be checked directly:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:moments}
 \sum_{j=1}^s a_{ij}c_j^{\ell-1}=\frac{c_i^\ell}{\ell}
 \quad(1\le\ell\le p-1),\qquad
 \sum_{i=1}^s b_i c_i^{\ell-1}=\frac1\ell
 \quad(1\le\ell\le p).
\end{equation}
The first group is empty if $p=1$. In the standard notation these conditions
are $C(p-1)$ and $B(p)$ \cite{HO_HairerWanner1996,HO_AkrivisLiLi2019}.
They distinguish the temporal result below from an ordinary differential
equation order statement. For an $s$-stage Gauss method, the present proof
allows $p\le\min\{s+1,2s\}$; for Radau IIA it allows
$p\le\min\{s+1,2s-1\}$. Thus backward Euler covers $p=1$, and the
$(p-1)$-stage Gauss method covers every $p\ge2$. This gives a construction of
arbitrary temporal order without a spatial discretization.

\begin{theorem}[Order-$p$ temporal convergence]\label{thm:ho:convergence}
Assume \eqref{eq:ho:rk-assumptions}, \eqref{eq:ho:moments}, and
\cref{lem:forms,lem:smooth-factor}. Let $\bs x$ solve \eqref{eq:abstract}, with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:regularity}
 \bs x\in C^{p+1}([0,T];\V)\cap C^1([0,T];\mathcal R),
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal R$ is defined in \eqref{eq:regularity-norm}. Assume that the
equations hold at the stage times and that
$\bs x^0,\ldots,\bs x^{p-1}\in\mathfrak K$. Set
$\epsilon_{\rm in}=\max_{0\le j<p}\|\bs x(t_j)-\bs x^j\|_{\DD}$.
Then the solution of \eqref{eq:ho:scheme} satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:global-error}
 \max_{0\le n\le N}\|\bs x(t_n)-\bs x^n\|_{\DD}^2
 +\dt\sum_{n=p-1}^{N-1}\sum_i b_i
       \|\bs x(t_{n,i})-\bs x_{n,i}\|_{\Aform}^2
 \le C\bigl(\epsilon_{\rm in}^2+\dt^{2p}\bigr).
\end{equation}
The constant depends on the fixed coefficients, $p$, $T$, the physical
parameters, the domain, and the bounds in \eqref{eq:ho:regularity}, but not on
$\dt$. In particular, $\epsilon_{\rm in}=O(\dt^p)$ gives order $p$ in the stated
norms. 
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Write $x_n=\bs x(t_n)$ and $x_{n,i}=\bs x(t_{n,i})$. Introduce reference stages
that satisfy the Runge--Kutta stage relations exactly:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:reference-stages}
 U_{n,i}=\bs x(t_n)+\dt\sum_{j=1}^s a_{ij}\partial_t\bs x(t_{n,j}),\qquad
 d_n=\bs x(t_{n+1}) - \bs x(t_n)-\dt\sum_{i=1}^s b_i\partial_t\bs x(t_{n,i}).
\end{equation}
They lie in $\mathfrak K$. Let
$\widehat{\bs x}(t_{n,i})=\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}\ell_j(c_i)\bs x(t_{n-j})$.
Taylor expansion in $\V$, using \eqref{eq:ho:moments}, gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:reference-bounds}
 \max_i\|U_{n,i}-\bs x(t_{n,i})\|_{\V}
 +\max_i\|\widehat{\bs x}(t_{n,i})-\bs x(t_{n,i})\|_{\V}\le C\dt^p,
 \qquad \|d_n\|_{\DD}\le C\dt^{p+1}.
\end{equation}
%For example, the coefficient of $\dt^\ell\partial_t^\ell\bs x(t_n)$ in the
%stage formula is $\sum_j a_{ij}c_j^{\ell-1}/(\ell-1)!$; the first group in
%\eqref{eq:ho:moments} makes it equal to $c_i^\ell/\ell!$ through degree
%$p-1$. The quadrature conditions give the endpoint bound. The same Taylor
%argument gives the extrapolation bound. 
Moreover \eqref{eq:ho:regularity} implies
$\|U_{n,i}\|_{\mathcal R}\le C$.

Set $e^n=\bs x(t_n)-\bs x^n$, $E_{n,i}=U_{n,i}-\bs x_{n,i}$,
$D_{n,i}=\partial_t\bs x(t_{n,i})-\mathcal K_{n,i}$, and
$\widehat e_{n,i}=\widehat{\bs x}(t_{n,i})-\widehat{\bs x}_{n,i}$.
Then
\[
 E_{n,i}=e^n+\dt\sum_{j=1}^s a_{ij}D_{n,j}\in \mathfrak K,\qquad
 e^{n+1}-d_n=e^n+\dt\sum_{i=1}^s b_iD_{n,i} \in \mathfrak K.
\]
Subtraction of the
numerical and reference equations with test function $\bs y \in \mathfrak K$, gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:error-equation}
\begin{aligned}
 \DD(D_{n,i},\bs y)&+\Aform(E_{n,i},\bs y)  +\JJ(E_{n,i},\bs y)
       +\BB(\widehat{\bs x}_{n,i},E_{n,i},\bs y)=R_{n,i}(\bs y)-\BB(\widehat e_{n,i},U_{n,i},\bs y),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
whith
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:residual}
\begin{aligned}
 R_{n,i}(\bs y)={}&\Aform(U_{n,i}-\bs x(t_{n,i}),\bs y) +\JJ(U_{n,i} - \bs x(t_{n,i}),\bs y)\\
 {}& +\BB(\widehat{\bs x}(t_{n,i}),U_{n,i},\bs y)
       -\BB(\bs x(t_{n,i}),\bs x(t_{n,i}),\bs y).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Split the last line as
$$
\BB(\widehat{\bs x}(t_{n,i}),U_{n,i}-\bs x(t_{n,i}),\bs y)
 +\BB(\widehat{\bs x}(t_{n,i})-\bs x(t_{n,i}),\bs x(t_{n,i}),\bs y).
 $$
\cref{eq:ho:reference-bounds} implies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:residual-bound}
 \|R_{n,i}\|_{\Aform^*}\le C\dt^p.
\end{equation}
%
%Set $e^n=x_n-\bs x^n$, $E_{n,i}=U_{n,i}-\bs x_{n,i}$,
%$D_{n,i}=\partial_t\bs x(t_{n,i})-\mathcal K_{n,i}$, and
%$\widehat e_{n,i}=\widehat{\bs x}_{n,i}-\widehat{\bs x}_{n,i}$.
%Then
%\[
% E_{n,i}=e^n+k\sum_j a_{ij}D_{n,j},\qquad
% e^{n+1}-d_n=e^n+k\sum_i b_iD_{n,i}.
%\]
%All these state differences lie in $\mathfrak K$. Subtraction of the
%numerical and reference equations, tested on $\mathfrak K$, gives
%\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:error-equation}
%\begin{aligned}
% \DD(D_{n,i},\bs y)&+(\Aform+\JJ)(E_{n,i},\bs y)
%       +\BB(\widehat{\bs x}_{n,i},E_{n,i},\bs y)\\
% &=R_{n,i}(\bs y)-\BB(\widehat e_{n,i},U_{n,i},\bs y).
%\end{aligned}
%\end{equation}
Take $\bs y=E_{n,i}\in\mathfrak K$ in \eqref{eq:ho:error-equation} and apply the quadratic identity
\eqref{eq:ho:quadratic-identity} to these error relations. Drop the
nonnegative $\mathsf G$ term and use \cref{lem:smooth-factor} with the smooth
argument $U_{n,i}$. If
$M_n=\max_{0\le j\le n}\|e^j\|_{\DD}$, then
$\|\widehat e_{n,i}\|_{\DD}\le C M_n$, and Young's inequality gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:error-energy}
 \|e^{n+1}-d_n\|_{\DD}^2
 +\dt\sum_{i=1}^s b_i\|E_{n,i}\|_{\Aform}^2
 \le\|e^n\|_{\DD}^2+C\dt M_n^2+C\dt^{2p+1}.
\end{equation}
Using the following inequality
\[
 \|e^{n+1}\|_{\DD}^2
 \le(1+\dt)\|e^{n+1}-d_n\|_{\DD}^2
                  +(1+\dt^{-1})\|d_n\|_{\DD}^2.
\]
Since $\dt\le T$, \eqref{eq:ho:reference-bounds} and
\eqref{eq:ho:error-energy} yield
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:error-recurrence}
 \|e^{n+1}\|_{\DD}^2-\|e^n\|_{\DD}^2
 +\dt\sum_{i=1}^s b_i\|E_{n,i}\|_{\Aform}^2
 \le C\dt M_n^2+C\dt^{2p+1}.
\end{equation}
Thus $M_{n+1}^2\le(1+C\dt)M_n^2+C\dt^{2p+1}$, and iteration bounds the
endpoint error by $C(\epsilon_{in}^2+\dt^{2p})$. Summing
\eqref{eq:ho:error-recurrence} bounds the accumulated stage error. Finally,
$\bs x(t_{n,i})-\bs x_{n,i}=E_{n,i}+ \bs x(t_{n,i})-U_{n,i}$ and
\eqref{eq:ho:reference-bounds} give \eqref{eq:ho:global-error}.
\end{proof}

%{\color{red}
%\begin{remark}[Stage order and classical order]\label{rem:ho:orders}
%The conditions \eqref{eq:ho:moments} are sufficient, not necessary. They are
%used to prove a temporal error estimate in the continuous spaces. For example,
%a two-stage Gauss method has classical order four, but it has stage order two;
%the theorem above guarantees at most order three for that choice. This does not
%rule out order four under stronger compatibility and operator estimates.
%A three-stage Gauss method with fourth-order extrapolation satisfies all the
%conditions for the order-four result proved here. Endpoint or accumulated
%stage estimates do not, by themselves, give the same order for stage pressures.
%\end{remark}
%}

\subsection{Starting values}
One sufficient linear start uses time derivatives of compatible initial data.
Suppose $\partial_t^k\bs x(0)\in\mathfrak K$ are available for
$0\le k\le p-1$. In each of the first $p-1$ steps, replace
\eqref{eq:ho:predictor} by the prescribed polynomial
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:Taylor-start}
 Z_{n,i}^{\rm start}=\sum_{k=0}^{p-1}
                 \frac{t_{n,i}^{k}}{k!}\partial_t^k\bs x(0),
 \qquad 0\le n\le p-2,
\end{equation}
and otherwise use \eqref{eq:ho:scheme}. For $p=1$ no starting step is needed.

\begin{proposition}[A time-only linear start]\label{prop:ho:time-start}
Under \cref{thm:ho:convergence}, let $\bs x^0=\bs x(0)$ and use
\eqref{eq:ho:Taylor-start} in the first $p-1$ steps. Then the resulting
starting values satisfy $\epsilon_{\rm in}\le C\dt^p$. Every starting step is
linear, uniquely solvable, and satisfies \eqref{eq:ho:energy-identity}.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Taylor's formula gives
$\|Z_{n,i}^{\rm start}-\bs x(t_{n,i})\|_{\V}\le C\dt^p$, since
$0\le t_{n,i}\le(p-1)\dt$ and $p$ is fixed. In the proof of
\cref{thm:ho:convergence}, use this same prescribed predictor for the
reference and numerical starting equations. The predictor-error term in
\eqref{eq:ho:error-equation} is then absent, and the residual remains
$O(\dt^p)$ in $\Aform^*$ norm. The same energy recurrence, starting with $e^0=0$,
gives the stated bound over the first $p-1$ steps.
Solvability and the energy identity follow from
\cref{thm:ho:solvability,thm:ho:energy-solvability}.
\end{proof}
The derivatives in this construction are a compatibility requirement on the
initial data, not extra unknown exact values at later times. When they exist,
they can be characterized recursively, for $0\le k\le p-2$, by differentiating \eqref{eq:abstract}:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:initial-derivatives}
\begin{aligned}
 \DD(\partial_t^{k+1}\bs x(0),\bs y)+\CC(\bs y,\partial_t^k\pp(0))
 ={}&\dual{\partial_t^k\ff(0)}{\bs y}-\Aform(\partial_t^k\bs x(0),\bs y) - \JJ(\partial_t\bs x(0),\bs y) \\
 &-\sum_{r=0}^{k}\binom{k}{r}\BB(\partial_t^r\bs x(0),\partial_t^{k-r}\bs x(0),\bs y),& & \forall~ \bs y \in \V, \\
 \CC(\partial_t^{k+1}\bs x(0),q)={} & 0, & & \forall~q \in W.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The right-hand side uses only lower derivatives. Membership of these derivatives
in $\V$ is not automatic for general data. The convergence theorem can instead
be used with any supplied order-$p$ starting values. 
%{\color{red}{A practical derivative-free
%linear correction procedure after finite element discretization is given in
%\cref{subsec:ho:FE-start}; its fixed-mesh accuracy argument is kept there.}}



\subsection{A first-order example: the semi-implicit backward Euler scheme}
\label{sec:BE}

The first-order semi-implicit scheme is contained in the general
Runge--Kutta framework \eqref{eq:ho:scheme}. It is obtained from the
one-stage Radau IIA method by choosing
\begin{equation}\label{eq:BE-RK-coefficients}
 s=1,\qquad
 p=1,\qquad
 \mathsf A=(a_{11})=(1),\qquad
 \bs b=(b_1)=(1),\qquad
 \bs c=(c_1)=(1).
\end{equation}
The corresponding Butcher tableau is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:BE-tableau}
\begin{array}{c|c}
1&1\\
\hline
&1
\end{array}.
\end{equation}
Here \(s=1\) means that there is one stage, while \(p=1\) is the
extrapolation order and the resulting temporal order.

For \(p=1\), the predictor \eqref{eq:ho:predictor} contains only the
term \(j=0\). Since the corresponding product is empty,
\[
 \ell_0(c)=1.
\]
Therefore,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:BE-predictor}
 \widehat{\bs x}_{n,1}=\bs x^n.
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\[
 t_{n,1}=t_n+c_1\dt=t_{n+1}.
\]
The matrix in the algebraic stability condition is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:BE-G}
 \mathsf G=(g_{11}),\qquad
 g_{11}=2b_1a_{11}-b_1^2=1,
\end{equation}
and hence
\[
 \mathsf G=(1)\succeq0.
\]
Thus all assumptions in \eqref{eq:ho:rk-assumptions} are satisfied.
The stage condition \(C(p-1)=C(0)\) is empty, while the quadrature
condition \(B(1)\) reduces to \(b_1=1\).

With the coefficients in \eqref{eq:BE-RK-coefficients}, the stage
relation and the endpoint update in \eqref{eq:ho:scheme} become
\[
 \bs x_{n,1}=\bs x^n+\dt\mathcal K_{n,1},
 \qquad
 \bs x^{n+1}=\bs x^n+\dt\mathcal K_{n,1}.
\]
Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:BE-stage-identification}
 \bs x_{n,1}=\bs x^{n+1},\qquad
 \mathcal K_{n,1}
 =\frac{\bs x^{n+1}-\bs x^n}{\dt}
 =\delta_t\bs x^{n+1},\qquad
 P_{n,1}=\pp^{\,n+1}.
\end{equation}
For $0 \le n\le N-1$, substitution of
\eqref{eq:BE-predictor}--\eqref{eq:BE-stage-identification}
into the general stage equation gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:first-order-eq}
\begin{aligned}
 \DD(\delta_t\bs x^{n+1},\bs y)
 +\Aform(\bs x^{n+1},\bs y)
 +\JJ(\bs x^{n+1},\bs y)
 +\BB(\bs x^n,\bs x^{n+1},\bs y)
 +\CC(\bs y,\pp^{\,n+1})
 &=
 \dual{\ff(t_{n+1})}{\bs y}, & & \forall~\bs y \in \V,\\
 \CC(\bs x^{n+1},q)&=0, & & \forall~q \in W,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
%After replacing \(n+1\) by \(n\), this becomes the first-order
%semi-implicit scheme: given \(\bs x^{n-1}\), find
%\((\bs x^n,\pp^n)\in\V\times W\) such that
%\begin{equation}\label{eq:BE}
%\begin{aligned}
% \DD(\delta_t\bs x^n,\bs y)
% +\Aform(\bs x^n,\bs y)
% +\JJ(\bs x^n,\bs y)
% +\BB(\bs x^{n-1},\bs x^n,\bs y)
% +\CC(\bs y,\pp^n)
% &=
% \dual{\ff(t_n)}{\bs y},
% &&\bs y\in\V,\\
% \CC(\bs x^n,q)&=0,
% &&q\in W,
%\end{aligned}
%\end{equation}
where
\[
 \delta_t\bs x^n=\frac{\bs x^n-\bs x^{n-1}}{\dt}.
\]
%Thus the nonlinear coefficient is evaluated at the known state
%\(\bs x^{n-1}\), whereas the second argument of \(\BB\) is treated

\subsection{A second-order example: the extrapolated Crank--Nicolson scheme}
\label{sec:CN}

The second-order extrapolated Crank--Nicolson scheme is a special case
of the general Runge--Kutta formulation \eqref{eq:ho:scheme}. It is
obtained from the one-stage Gauss method by choosing
\begin{equation}\label{eq:CN-RK-coefficients}
 s=1,\qquad
 p=2,\qquad
 \mathsf A=(a_{11})=\left(\frac12\right),\qquad
 \bs b=(b_1)=(1),\qquad
 \bs c=(c_1)=\left(\frac12\right).
\end{equation}
The corresponding Butcher tableau is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:CN-tableau}
\begin{array}{c|c}
\frac12&\frac12\\
\hline
&1
\end{array}.
\end{equation}
The underlying Runge--Kutta method is the implicit midpoint rule. It
has classical order two and stage order one.

For \(p=2\), the extrapolation basis functions in
\eqref{eq:ho:predictor} are
\[
 \ell_0(c)=c+1,
 \qquad
 \ell_1(c)=-c.
\]
Since \(c_1=1/2\), the predictor is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:CN-predictor-RK}
 \widehat{\bs x}_{n,1}
 =
 \ell_0\left(\frac12\right)\bs x^n
 +
 \ell_1\left(\frac12\right)\bs x^{n-1}
 =
 \frac32\bs x^n-\frac12\bs x^{n-1}.
\end{equation}
The algebraic stability matrix contains only one entry:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:CN-G}
 \mathsf G=(g_{11}),
 \qquad
 g_{11}
 =
 2b_1a_{11}-b_1^2
 =
 2\cdot1\cdot\frac12-1
 =
 0.
\end{equation}
Hence
\[
 \mathsf G=(0)\succeq0.
\]
The order conditions required for \(p=2\) also hold:
\[
 a_{11}=c_1=\frac12,
 \qquad
 b_1=1,
 \qquad
 b_1c_1=\frac12.
\]
These are precisely the conditions \(C(1)\) and \(B(2)\).

With the coefficients \eqref{eq:CN-RK-coefficients}, the stage relation
and endpoint update in \eqref{eq:ho:scheme} become
\[
 \bs x_{n,1}
 =
 \bs x^n+\frac{\dt}{2}\mathcal K_{n,1},
 \qquad
 \bs x^{n+1}
 =
 \bs x^n+\dt\mathcal K_{n,1}.
\]
Therefore,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:CN-stage-identification}
 \bs x_{n,1}
 =
 \frac{\bs x^{n+1}+\bs x^n}{2},
 \qquad
 \mathcal K_{n,1}
 =
 \frac{\bs x^{n+1}-\bs x^n}{\dt},
 \qquad
 P_{n,1}=\pp^{\,n+1/2}.
\end{equation}
Introduce
\begin{equation}\label{eq:CN-RK-notation}
 \bar{\bs x}^{\,n+1}
 :=
 \frac{\bs x^{n+1}+\bs x^n}{2},
 \qquad
 \widehat{\bs x}^{\,n}
 :=
 \frac32\bs x^n-\frac12\bs x^{n-1},
 \qquad
 t_{n+1/2}:=t_n+\frac{\dt}{2}.
\end{equation}
Then
\[
 \bs x_{n,1}=\bar{\bs x}^{\,n+1},
 \qquad
 \mathcal K_{n,1}=\delta_t\bs x^{n+1},
 \qquad
 \widehat{\bs x}_{n,1}=\widehat{\bs x}^{\,n}.
\]

For $0\le n\le N-1$, substitution into the general stage equation \eqref{eq:ho:scheme} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:second-order-eq}
\begin{aligned}
 \DD(\delta_t\bs x^{n+1},\bs y)
 +\Aform(\bar{\bs x}^{\,n+1},\bs y)
 +\JJ(\bar{\bs x}^{\,n+1},\bs y) & \\
 +\BB(\widehat{\bs x}^{\,n},\bar{\bs x}^{\,n+1},\bs y)
 +\CC(\bs y,\pp^{\,n+1/2})
 &=
 \dual{\ff(t_{n+1/2})}{\bs y},&& \forall~\bs y \in \V,\\
 \CC(\bar{\bs x}^{\,n+1},q)&=0, & & \forall~q \in W.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
%After replacing \(n+1\) by \(n\), define
%\begin{equation}\label{eq:CN-notation}
% \bar{\bs x}^{\,n}
% =
% \frac{\bs x^n+\bs x^{n-1}}2,
% \qquad
% \widehat{\bs x}^{\,n-1}
% =
% \frac{3\bs x^{n-1}-\bs x^{n-2}}2,
% \qquad
% t_{n-1/2}
% =
% \frac{t_n+t_{n-1}}2.
%\end{equation}
%The resulting scheme is: for \(n\ge2\), given
%\(\bs x^{n-1},\bs x^{n-2}\in\mathfrak K\), find
%\((\bs x^n,\pp^{n-1/2})\in\V\times W\) such that
%\begin{equation}\label{eq:CN}
%\begin{aligned}
% \DD(\delta_t\bs x^n,\bs y)
% +\Aform(\bar{\bs x}^{\,n},\bs y)
% +\JJ(\bar{\bs x}^{\,n},\bs y)
% +\BB(\widehat{\bs x}^{\,n-1},\bar{\bs x}^{\,n},\bs y)
% +\CC(\bs y,\pp^{n-1/2})
% &=
% \dual{\ff(t_{n-1/2})}{\bs y},
% &&\bs y\in\V,\\
% \CC(\bar{\bs x}^{\,n},q)&=0,
% &&q\in W,
%\end{aligned}
%\end{equation}
%where
%\[
% \delta_t\bs x^n
% =
% \frac{\bs x^n-\bs x^{n-1}}{k}.
%\]
The first argument of \(\BB\) is known, while its second argument is
the current midpoint state. Hence \eqref{eq:second-order-eq} is linear at every
step once the starting value \(\bs x^1\) is available. The pressure
\(\pp^{n+1/2}\) is an independent midpoint multiplier; it is not
defined as an average of endpoint pressures.

%\paragraph{Starting value.}
The two-step extrapolation requires a second-order accurate value
\(\bs x^1\). One possible choice is the nonlinear midpoint start:
find \((\bs x^1,\pp^{1/2})\in\V\times W\) such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:CN-start}
\begin{aligned}
 \DD(\delta_t\bs x^1,\bs y)
 +\Aform(\bar{\bs x}^{\,1},\bs y)
 +\JJ(\bar{\bs x}^{\,1},\bs y)
 +\BB(\bar{\bs x}^{\,1},\bar{\bs x}^{\,1},\bs y)
 +\CC(\bs y,\pp^{1/2})
 &=
 \dual{\ff(t_{1/2})}{\bs y},
 &&\bs y\in\V,\\
 \CC(\bar{\bs x}^{\,1},q)&=0,
 &&q\in W,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where
\[
 \bar{\bs x}^{\,1}
 =
 \frac{\bs x^1+\bs x^0}{2}.
\]
The starting problem \eqref{eq:CN-start} is nonlinear. In the
time-semidiscrete analysis, a solution is assumed to be available.
Its existence after spatial discretization is treated separately.
Alternatively, the linear starting procedure in
\cref{prop:ho:time-start} gives a second-order starting value when the
required compatible initial derivative is available.

\subsection{A third-order time-semidiscrete Gauss-type example}
\label{subsec:ho:third-order-example}

We now construct a third-order linearly implicit method within the
general framework \eqref{eq:ho:scheme}. The temporal convergence
theorem with \(p=3\) requires the stage condition \(C(2)\), the
quadrature condition \(B(3)\), and a third-order extrapolation. The
minimal Gauss method satisfying these conditions is the two-stage
Gauss method. We therefore choose
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:order-three-parameters}
 s=2,\qquad p=3.
\end{equation}

The Runge--Kutta coefficients are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:order-three-coefficients}
 \mathsf A=
 \begin{pmatrix}
 \dfrac14&
 \dfrac14-\dfrac{\sqrt3}{6}
 \\[2mm]
 \dfrac14+\dfrac{\sqrt3}{6}&
 \dfrac14
 \end{pmatrix},
 \qquad
 \bs b=
 \begin{pmatrix}
 \dfrac12\\[1mm]\dfrac12
 \end{pmatrix},
 \qquad
 \bs c=
 \begin{pmatrix}
 \dfrac12-\dfrac{\sqrt3}{6}\\[1mm]
 \dfrac12+\dfrac{\sqrt3}{6}
 \end{pmatrix},\qquad
 \mathsf G = 0.
\end{equation}
%Equivalently, the Butcher tableau is
%\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:order-three-tableau}
%\begin{array}{c|cc}
%\displaystyle\frac12-\frac{\sqrt3}{6}
%&
%\displaystyle\frac14
%&
%\displaystyle\frac14-\frac{\sqrt3}{6}
%\\[2mm]
%\displaystyle\frac12+\frac{\sqrt3}{6}
%&
%\displaystyle\frac14+\frac{\sqrt3}{6}
%&
%\displaystyle\frac14
%\\
%\hline
%&
%\displaystyle\frac12
%&
%\displaystyle\frac12
%\end{array}.
%\end{equation}
%
The two-stage Gauss method has classical order four and stage order
two. Its coefficients satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:order-three-moments}
 \mathsf A\bs 1=\bs c,
 \qquad
 \mathsf A\bs c
 =
 \frac12
 \begin{pmatrix}
 c_1^2\\ c_2^2
 \end{pmatrix},
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:order-three-quadrature}
 \bs b^{\mathsf T}\bs 1=1,
 \qquad
 \bs b^{\mathsf T}\bs c=\frac12,
 \qquad
 \bs b^{\mathsf T}
 \begin{pmatrix}
 c_1^2\\ c_2^2
 \end{pmatrix}
 =\frac13.
\end{equation}
Thus the conditions \(C(2)\) and \(B(3)\) in
\eqref{eq:ho:moments} hold.

%Moreover,
%\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:order-three-G}
% \mathsf G=(g_{ij})_{i,j=1}^2=\mathsf 0,
% \qquad
% g_{ij}=b_i a_{ij}+b_j a_{ji}-b_i b_j.
%\end{equation}
%Indeed,
%\[
% g_{11}=g_{22}=0,
% \qquad
% g_{12}=g_{21}
% =
% \frac12
% \left(
% \frac14-\frac{\sqrt3}{6}
% +
% \frac14+\frac{\sqrt3}{6}
% \right)
% -\frac14
% =0.
%\]
%Hence the method has no additional Runge--Kutta dissipation.

For \(p=3\), the Lagrange extrapolation basis functions are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:order-three-basis}
 \ell_0(c)=\frac{(c+1)(c+2)}2,
 \qquad
 \ell_1(c)=-c(c+2),
 \qquad
 \ell_2(c)=\frac{c(c+1)}2.
\end{equation}
The stage predictors are therefore
%\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:order-three-predictor}
% \widehat{\bs x}_{n,i}
% =
% \frac{(c_i+1)(c_i+2)}2\,\bs x^n
% -c_i(c_i+2)\,\bs x^{n-1}
% +\frac{c_i(c_i+1)}2\,\bs x^{n-2},
% \qquad i=1,2.
%\end{equation}
%Equivalently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:order-three-explicit-predictor}
\begin{aligned}
 \widehat{\bs x}_{n,1}
 ={}&
 \left(\frac{23}{12}-\frac{\sqrt3}{3}\right)\bs x^n
 +
 \left(-\frac43+\frac{\sqrt3}{2}\right)\bs x^{n-1}
 +
 \left(\frac5{12}-\frac{\sqrt3}{6}\right)\bs x^{n-2},
 \\
 \widehat{\bs x}_{n,2}
 ={}&
 \left(\frac{23}{12}+\frac{\sqrt3}{3}\right)\bs x^n
 +
 \left(-\frac43-\frac{\sqrt3}{2}\right)\bs x^{n-1}
 +
 \left(\frac5{12}+\frac{\sqrt3}{6}\right)\bs x^{n-2}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
%When exact historical values are inserted,
%\[
% \widehat{\bs x}_{n,i}
% =
% \bs x(t_n+c_i \dt)+O(\dt^3)
% \qquad\text{in }\V.
%\]
%
Given
\[
 \bs x^n,\bs x^{n-1},\bs x^{n-2}\in\mathfrak K,
\]
find
\[
 \bs x_{n,i},\mathcal K_{n,i}\in\V,
 \qquad
 P_{n,i}\in W,
 \qquad i=1,2,
\]
such that
\begin{subequations}\label{eq:ho:order-three-scheme}
\begin{align}
 \bs x_{n,1}
 &=
 \bs x^n
 +
 \dt\left[
 \frac14\mathcal K_{n,1}
 +
 \left(
 \frac14-\frac{\sqrt3}{6}
 \right)\mathcal K_{n,2}
 \right],
 \label{eq:ho:order-three-stage-one}
 \\
 \bs x_{n,2}
 &=
 \bs x^n
 +
 \dt\left[
 \left(
 \frac14+\frac{\sqrt3}{6}
 \right)\mathcal K_{n,1}
 +
 \frac14\mathcal K_{n,2}
 \right],
 \label{eq:ho:order-three-stage-two}
 \\
 \DD(\mathcal K_{n,i},\bs y)
 +\Aform(\bs x_{n,i},\bs y)
 +\JJ(\bs x_{n,i},\bs y)
 &+
 \BB(\widehat{\bs x}_{n,i},\bs x_{n,i},\bs y)
 +\CC(\bs y,P_{n,i})
% \notag\\
% &
 =
 \dual{\ff(t_{n,i})}{\bs y},
 \qquad
 \bs y\in\V,
% \qquad i=1,2,
 \label{eq:ho:order-three-stage-equation}
 \\
 \CC(\bs x_{n,i},q)
 &=0,
 \qquad
 q\in W,
% \qquad i=1,2,
 \label{eq:ho:order-three-constraint}
 \\
 \bs x^{n+1}
 &=
 \bs x^n
 +
 \frac{\dt}{2}
 \left(
 \mathcal K_{n,1}+\mathcal K_{n,2}
 \right).
 \label{eq:ho:order-three-update}
\end{align}
\end{subequations}
%where
%\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:order-three-stage-times}
% t_{n,1}
% =
% t_n+
% \left(
% \frac12-\frac{\sqrt3}{6}

% \qquad

% t_n+

% \frac12+\frac{\sqrt3}{6}
% \right)k.

\subsection{A fourth-order time-semidiscrete example}
\label{subsec:ho:fourth-order-example}

We now specialize the general Runge--Kutta formulation
\eqref{eq:ho:scheme} to a fourth-order linearly implicit method. To
satisfy the stage condition \(C(3)\) required by
\cref{thm:ho:convergence}, we choose the three-stage Gauss method and
set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:Gauss-four-parameters}
 s=3,\qquad p=4.
\end{equation}
The corresponding coefficients are
% Butcher tableau is
%\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:Gauss-three}
%\begin{array}{c|ccc}
%\displaystyle\frac12-\frac{\sqrt{15}}{10}
%&
%\displaystyle\frac5{36}
%&
%\displaystyle\frac29-\frac{\sqrt{15}}{15}
%&
%\displaystyle\frac5{36}-\frac{\sqrt{15}}{30}
%\\[2mm]
%\displaystyle\frac12
%&
%\displaystyle\frac5{36}+\frac{\sqrt{15}}{24}
%&
%\displaystyle\frac29
%&
%\displaystyle\frac5{36}-\frac{\sqrt{15}}{24}
%\\[2mm]
%\displaystyle\frac12+\frac{\sqrt{15}}{10}
%&
%\displaystyle\frac5{36}+\frac{\sqrt{15}}{30}
%&
%\displaystyle\frac29+\frac{\sqrt{15}}{15}
%&
%\displaystyle\frac5{36}
%\\
%\hline
%&
%\displaystyle\frac5{18}
%&
%\displaystyle\frac49
%&
%\displaystyle\frac5{18}
%\end{array}.
%\end{equation}
%Equivalently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:Gauss-three-coefficients}
\begin{gathered}
 \mathsf A=
 \begin{pmatrix}
 \dfrac5{36}
 &
 \dfrac29-\dfrac{\sqrt{15}}{15}
 &
 \dfrac5{36}-\dfrac{\sqrt{15}}{30}
 \\[2mm]
 \dfrac5{36}+\dfrac{\sqrt{15}}{24}
 &
 \dfrac29
 &
 \dfrac5{36}-\dfrac{\sqrt{15}}{24}
 \\[2mm]
 \dfrac5{36}+\dfrac{\sqrt{15}}{30}
 &
 \dfrac29+\dfrac{\sqrt{15}}{15}
 &
 \dfrac5{36}
 \end{pmatrix},\quad
% \\[2mm]
 \bs b=
 \begin{pmatrix}
 5/18\\[1mm]4/9\\[1mm]5/18
 \end{pmatrix},
 \quad
 \bs c=
 \begin{pmatrix}
 1/2-\sqrt{15}/10\\[1mm]
 1/2\\[1mm]
 1/2+\sqrt{15}/10
 \end{pmatrix},
 \quad \mathsf G = 0.
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
The three-stage Gauss method has classical order six and stage order
three. In particular, it satisfies \(C(3)\) and \(B(6)\), and hence
also satisfies the conditions \(C(3)\) and \(B(4)\) required for the
fourth-order result in \cref{thm:ho:convergence}
\cite{HO_HairerWanner1996}.
%
%The Gauss coefficients satisfy
%\[
% b_i a_{ij}+b_j a_{ji}=b_i b_j,
% \qquad 1\le i,j\le3.
%\]
%Therefore,
%\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:Gauss-three-G}
% \mathsf G=(g_{ij})_{i,j=1}^3=\mathsf 0.
%% \qquad
%% g_{ij}=b_i a_{ij}+b_j a_{ji}-b_i b_j.
%\end{equation}
%Thus the method has no additional Runge--Kutta dissipation term.

For \(p=4\), the extrapolated value at the \(i\)th stage is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:fourth-predictor}
\begin{aligned}
 \widehat{\bs x}_{n,i}
 ={}&
 \frac{(c_i+1)(c_i+2)(c_i+3)}6\,\bs x^n
 -\frac{c_i(c_i+2)(c_i+3)}2\,\bs x^{n-1}
 \\[1mm]
 &+
 \frac{c_i(c_i+1)(c_i+3)}2\,\bs x^{n-2}
 -\frac{c_i(c_i+1)(c_i+2)}6\,\bs x^{n-3},
 \qquad i=1,2,3.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
%This predictor is obtained from the four previous endpoint values and
%satisfies
%\[
% \widehat{\bs x}_{n,i}
% =
% \bs x(t_n+c_i \dt)+O(\dt^4).
%\]
%%when exact historical values are used.
%
Given
\[
 \bs x^n,\bs x^{n-1},\bs x^{n-2},\bs x^{n-3}
 \in\mathfrak K,
\]
find the stage states
\[
 \bs x_{n,i}\in\V,
 \qquad
 \mathcal K_{n,i}\in\V,
 \qquad
 P_{n,i}\in W,
 \qquad i=1,2,3,
\]
such that
\begin{subequations}\label{eq:ho:Gauss-four-scheme}
\begin{align}
 \bs x_{n,i}
 &=
 \bs x^n
 +\dt\sum_{j=1}^3a_{ij}\mathcal K_{n,j},
 \qquad i=1,2,3,
 \label{eq:ho:Gauss-four-stage}
 \\[1mm]
 \DD(\mathcal K_{n,i},\bs y)
 +\Aform(\bs x_{n,i},\bs y)
 +\JJ(\bs x_{n,i},\bs y)
 &+
 \BB(\widehat{\bs x}_{n,i},\bs x_{n,i},\bs y)
 +\CC(\bs y,P_{n,i})
% \notag\\
% &
 =
 \dual{\ff(t_{n,i})}{\bs y},
 \qquad
 \bs y\in\V,
 \label{eq:ho:Gauss-four-stage-equation}
 \\[1mm]
 \CC(\bs x_{n,i},q)
 &=0,
 \qquad
 q\in W,
 \qquad i=1,2,3,
 \label{eq:ho:Gauss-four-constraint}
 \\[1mm]
 \bs x^{n+1}
 &=
 \bs x^n
 +\dt\left(
 \frac5{18}\mathcal K_{n,1}
 +\frac49\mathcal K_{n,2}
 +\frac5{18}\mathcal K_{n,3}
 \right).
 \label{eq:ho:Gauss-four-update}
\end{align}
\end{subequations}
%where
%\[
% t_{n,i}=t_n+c_i \dt.
%\]
Since the predictors \(\widehat{\bs x}_{n,i}\) are known, the three
stages form one coupled linear variational problem. They are not three
independent one-stage problems.
%

\section{Finite element discretization}\label{sec:FE}
\subsection{Finite element spaces}
Let $\mathcal T_h$ be a conforming shape-regular tetrahedral mesh. For any integer $l\ge 1$, use the compatible sequence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:commuting-exact-sequence}
\begin{CD}
%H_0^1(\Omega)
%@>{\operatorname{grad}}>>
%\bs U
%@>{\operatorname{curl}}>>
%\bs V
%@>{\operatorname{div}}>>
%W
%\\
%@VV{\pi_h^i}V
%@VV{\pi_h^{ci}}V
%@VV{\pi_h^{di}}V
%@VV{Q_h^i}V
%\\
\Sigma_h
@>{\operatorname{grad}}>>
\bs U_h
@>{\operatorname{curl}}>>
\bs V_h
@>{\operatorname{div}}>>
W_h
\end{CD}
\end{equation}
%
%
%\begin{equation}\label{eq:discrete-sequence}
% \Sigma_h^{[k]}\rightarrow{\nabla}\bs U_h^i\bs x\rightarrow{\curl}\bs V_h^i
% \bs x\rightarrow{\Div}W_h^i,
%\end{equation}
with homogeneous traces as in the corresponding continuous spaces. Here $\Sigma_h$ contains continuous degree-$l$ scalar polynomials, $\bs U_h$ is the first-family N\'ed\'elec space of order $(l-1)$, $\bs V_h$ is the Raviart--Thomas space of order $(l-1)$, and $W_h$ contains discontinuous degree-$(l-1)$ polynomials of zero mean. 
%Polynomial order is indexed so that the lowest N\'ed\'elec and Raviart--Thomas spaces have order zero. 
These spaces fit the finite element de Rham complex \cite{Nedelec1980,RaviartThomas1977,ArnoldFalkWinther2006,ArnoldFalkWinther2010}.


For the velocity--pressure discretization, we employ stable mixed
finite element pairs. For the lowest-order case \(l=1\), we use the
mini element pair
\(
\bs S_h\times Q_h,
\)
where the velocity space consists of continuous piecewise linear
functions enriched with element bubble functions, and the pressure
space consists of continuous piecewise linear functions with zero
mean \cite{Arnoldmini1984}. 
For higher-order cases \(l\ge2\), we adopt the classical Taylor--Hood
finite element pair \cite{TaylorHood1973}:
\[
\bs S_h
=
\left\{
\boldsymbol v_h\in [C^0(\overline\Omega)]^d:
\boldsymbol v_h|_K\in [\mathbb P_l(K)]^d,
\ \boldsymbol v_h=0\ \text{on }\partial\Omega
\right\},
\]
and
\[
Q_h
=
\left\{
q_h\in C^0(\overline\Omega):
q_h|_K\in\mathbb P_{l-1}(K),
\ \int_\Omega q_h\,dx=0
\right\}.
\]
%Here, \(\bs S_h^{[k]}\) and \(Q_h^{[k]}\) denote the continuous
%velocity and pressure spaces of polynomial degrees \(k\) and \(k-1\),
%respectively. 
The pair
\((\bs S_h,Q_h)\) satisfies the discrete
Ladyzhenskaya--Babuška--Brezzi (LBB) condition: there exists a constant
\(c_0>0\), independent of the mesh size \(h\), such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:discrete-infsup}
 \inf_{q_h\in Q_h\setminus\{0\}}
 \sup_{\bs v_h\in\bs S_h\setminus\{0\}}
 \frac{|(\Div\bs v_h,q_h)|}
 {\|\nabla\bs v_h\|_{0}\|q_h\|_{0}}
 \ge c_0 .
\end{equation}
%
%
%
Define the weak curl $\curl_h:\bs V_h \to \bs U_h$ and the discrete Laplacian $L_h: \Sigma_h \to \Sigma_h$ by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:weak-operators}
\begin{aligned}
 (\curl_h\bs F_h,\bs\lambda_h)&=(\bs F_h,\curl\bs\lambda_h),
 &&\bs\lambda_h\in\bs U_h,\\
 (L_h\psi_h,\gamma_h)&=(\nabla\psi_h,\nabla\gamma_h),
 &&\gamma_h\in\Sigma_h.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
%Both operators are well defined by finite-dimensional mass matrices. Also let $\Pi_h^c$ and $\Pi_h^\Sigma$ be the $L^2$ projections onto $\bs U_h^i$ and $\Sigma_h^i$. Unlike the continuous projections discussed earlier, these projections exist because their ranges are finite-dimensional and closed. The construction itself does not require a canonical nodal interpolant on all of $H^1$. When approximation operators are used in a separate spatial error analysis, their domains and commuting properties must be specified; bounded commuting projections are a suitable option \cite{ArnoldFalkWinther2010}.

The discrete product space is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Vh}
 \V_h=\bs S_h\times(\Sigma_h)^3\times\bs V_h\times\Sigma_h.
\end{equation}
For any $\bs y_h = (\bs v_h,\bs s_h,\bs F_h,\psi_h)^\intercal \in \V_h$, its norm is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Vh-norm}
\begin{aligned}
 \|\bs y_h\|_{\V_h}^2={}&\|\nabla\bs v_h\|^2+\|\nabla\bs s_h\|^2
 +\|\bs F_h\|^2+\|\Div\bs F_h\|^2+\|\curl_h\bs F_h\|^2
% \\
% &
 +\|\nabla\psi_h\|^2+\|L_h\psi_h\|^2.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
In general $\V_h\not\subset\V$: Raviart--Thomas fields need not belong to $H(\curl)$, and continuous piecewise linear or quadratic potentials need not have a global $L^2$ Laplacian. The weak operators are therefore essential, not merely notational replacements.

Choose the initial velocity in the discrete divergence-free space, for example by its $L^2$ projection onto that space. Choose stated finite element approximations $\bs\omega_h^0$ and $\bs m_h^0$; their $L^2$ projections onto the indicated spaces are well defined. Determine the initial potential $\varphi_h\in \Sigma_h$ by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:discrete-phi0}
 \mu_0(\nabla\varphi_h^0,\nabla\psi_h)
 =-(\mu_0\bs m_h^0+\bs H_e(0),\nabla\psi_h),\qquad\psi_h\in\Sigma_h.
\end{equation}
%This makes the weak magnetostatic residual zero initially. It does not impose that residual at subsequent time steps.

\subsection{The discrete forms and schemes}
The bilinear forms $\DD$, $\JJ$, and $\CC$ remain unchanged. Define $\Aform_h$ from \eqref{eq:A} by replacing the magnetization curls by $\curl_h$ and the potential Laplacians by $-L_h$. Define $\BB_h$ from \eqref{eq:B} with the same replacements. 
%In particular, its final pair is
%\begin{equation}\label{eq:Bh-last-pair}
% \frac{\mu_0}{2}\bigl[(L_h\varphi_h,\bs v_h\cdot\bs A_h)
%                      -(L_h\psi_h,\bs u_h\cdot\bs A_h)\bigr].
%\end{equation}
%Its divergence pair retains the coefficient $\mu_0/2$. All products are integrated over the elements. Exact integration is assumed in the proofs. A quadrature implementation retains the conclusions only if it preserves every paired cancellation and gives positive definite mass and dissipative forms with the required norm equivalences.

\begin{lemma}[Discrete structure]\label{lem:discrete-forms}
For $\bs x_h,\bs y_h,\bs z_h\in\V_h$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:discrete-structure}
 \Aform_h(\bs y_h,\bs y_h)\simeq\|\bs y_h\|_{\V_h}^2,
 \qquad \BB_h(\bs z_h,\bs x_h,\bs y_h)=-\BB_h(\bs z_h,\bs y_h,\bs x_h).
\end{equation}
The constants in the energy-norm equivalence depend on the physical parameters, not on $h$. For fixed $h$, $\BB_h$ is a bounded trilinear form.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Replace the curls and Laplacians in \eqref{eq:A-square} by their weak counterparts. This gives the norm equivalence directly. Skew-symmetry follows from the paired terms and does not require integration by parts or an inverse estimate. Boundedness at a fixed mesh follows from finite dimensionality. 
%No mesh-independent nonlinear continuity estimate is inferred from finite dimensionality.
\end{proof}

The choices $i=1$ and $i=2$ match the two element families of the original manuscript; the stability arguments do not depend on this particular pairing of spatial degree and time order.

%\begin{currentchange}
%\paragraph{The higher-order fully discrete scheme.}
For a fix integer $l\ge 1$, define 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:FE-kernel}
 \mathfrak K_h=\{\bs y_h\in\V_h:\CC(\bs y_h,q_h)=0
                             \text{ for all }q_h\in Q_h\}.
\end{equation}
The fully discrete $p$-order temporal scheme reads as: Given $\bs x_h^0,\ldots,\bs x_h^{p-1}\in\mathfrak K_h$, use the coefficients
and extrapolation weights from \cref{sec:ho:arbitrary-order}:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:FE-predictor}
 \widehat{\bs x}_{h,n,i}=\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}\ell_j(c_i)\bs x_h^{n-j}.
\end{equation}
For $n=p-1,\ldots,N-1$, find
$\bs x_{h,n,i},\mathcal K_{h,n,i}\in\V_h$ and $P_{h,n,i}\in Q_h$ such that
\begin{subequations}\label{eq:ho:FE-scheme}
\begin{align}
 \bs x_{h,n,i}&=\bs x_h^n+\dt\sum_{j=1}^s a_{ij}\mathcal K_{h,n,j},\\
 \label{eq:ho:FE-scheme-b}
 \DD(\mathcal K_{h,n,i},\bs y_h)+\Aform_h(\bs x_{h,n,i},\bs y_h)
 &+\JJ(\bs x_{h,n,i},\bs y_h)
   +\BB_h(\widehat{\bs x}_{h,n,i},\bs x_{h,n,i},\bs y_h)
  \notag\\
 &
 +\CC(\bs y_h,P_{h,n,i})=\dual{\ff(t_{n,i})}{\bs y_h},\qquad\forall~\bs y_h\in \V_h,\\
\label{eq:ho:FE-scheme-c} \CC(\bs x_{h,n,i},q_h)&=0,\qquad\forall~q_h\in Q_h,\qquad i=1,\ldots,s,\\
 \bs x_h^{n+1}&=\bs x_h^n+\dt\sum_{i=1}^s b_i\mathcal K_{h,n,i}.
\end{align}
\end{subequations}
%All test functions are in $\V_h\times Q_h$. Use $E_h(U)=\tfrac12\DD(U,U)$.
%The replacements $\curl\bs m\mapsto K_h\bs m_h$ and
%$\Delta\varphi\mapsto-L_h\varphi_h$ are made in the forms before stage
%assembly. They preserve every paired cancellation, including
%\eqref{eq:Bh-last-pair}.
%\end{currentchange}

%\begin{currentchange}
\begin{theorem}[Fully discrete solvability and stability]\label{thm:fully-discrete}
Assume \eqref{eq:discrete-infsup} and the discrete structural properties.
%Backward Euler has a unique solution at every step, and the linear
%Crank--Nicolson steps are uniquely solvable for every $\dt>0$.
Under \eqref{eq:ho:rk-assumptions}, the higher-order system
\eqref{eq:ho:FE-scheme} has a unique solution for every $\dt>0$ and every
prescribed predictor in $\V_h$. It preserves $\mathfrak K_h$ and satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:FE-energy}
\begin{aligned}
 &E_h(\bs x_h^{n+1})-E_h(\bs x_h^n)
 +\dt\sum_{i=1}^s b_i\|\bs x_{h,n,i}\|_{\Aform_h}^2
% \\
% &\quad
 +\frac{\dt^2}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^sg_{ij}
       \DD(\mathcal K_{h,n,i},\mathcal K_{h,n,j})
 =\dt\sum_{i=1}^s b_i\dual{\ff(t_{n,i})}{\bs x_{h,n,i}}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:FE-forced}
 E_h(\bs x_h^{n+1})+\frac{\dt}{2}\sum_{i=1}^s b_i\|\bs x_{h,n,i}\|_{\Aform_h}^2
 \le E_h(\bs x_h^n)+\frac{\dt}{2}\sum_{i=1}^s b_i
                          \|\ff(t_{n,i})\|_{\Aform_h^*}^2.
\end{equation}
%The earlier backward Euler and Crank--Nicolson energy identities hold with
%the corresponding discrete forms. For a nonlinear midpoint start they
%hold for every selected solution. No energy estimate requires a relation
%between the mesh size and the time step.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
On $\mathfrak K_h^s$, use the weighted form
\eqref{eq:ho:product-form} with $\Aform_h$ and $\BB_h$. The same matrix
identity \eqref{eq:ho:inverse-positivity} gives coercivity, and the discrete
inf-sup condition recovers the pressures. The constraints also hold for
all stage derivatives and for the endpoint update.
The proof of \eqref{eq:ho:energy-identity} uses only the stage relations
and paired cancellations, so it gives \eqref{eq:ho:FE-energy} directly.
Young's inequality gives \eqref{eq:ho:FE-forced}. 
%The one-stage arguments
%establish the backward Euler and linear Crank--Nicolson statements.
\end{proof}
