%\subsection{A linear starting procedure}
%\label{subsec:ho:FE-start}

For the fully discrete $p$-order temporal scheme \eqref{eq:ho:FE-predictor}, one option for computing the initial values $\bs x_h^n$, $1\le n\le p-1$, is to use
$p$-step linear method~\cite{HO_SatoMiyatakeButcher2023}. That is, for \(0\le n \le p-1\), set
\(\bs x_{h,n,i}^{[0]}=\bs x_h^n\). For \(\ell=1,\ldots,p\), solve
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:FE-startup}
\begin{aligned}
 \bs x_{h,n,i}^{[\ell]}&=\bs x_h^n+\dt\sum_{j=1}^s a_{ij}\mathcal K_{h,n,j}^{[\ell]},\\
 \DD(\mathcal K_{h,n,i}^{[\ell]},\bs y_h)
 &+\Aform_h(\bs x_{h,n,i}^{[\ell]},\bs y_h)+\JJ(\bs x_{h,n,i}^{[\ell]},\bs y_h)\\
 &+\BB_h(\bs x_{h,n,i}^{[\ell-1]},\bs x_{h,n,i}^{[\ell]},\bs y_h)
   +\CC(\bs y_h,P_{h,n,i}^{[\ell]})=\dual{\ff(t_{n,i})}{\bs y_h},\qquad\forall~\bs y_h\in \V_h,\\
 \CC(\bs x_{h,n,i}^{[\ell]},q_h)&=0,\qquad\forall~q_h\in Q_h,
 \qquad i=1,\ldots,s.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
We then accept the update
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:FE-startup-update}
 \bs x_h^{n+1}=\bs x_h^n+\dt\sum_{i=1}^s b_i\mathcal K_{h,n,i}^{[p]}.
\end{equation}
This involves exactly \(p\) linear corrections, rather than a nonlinear solve with an unspecified stopping tolerance.


\subsection{Implementation of the fully discrete schemes}

The fully discrete \(p\)th-order scheme introduced in
\eqref{eq:ho:FE-predictor} involves the discrete adjoint operators
\(\curl_h\) and \(L_h\). For its implementation, these operators are
represented through auxiliary variables. At each Runge--Kutta stage, we write
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal K_{h,n,i}
&=
\bigl(
\mathcal K_{h,n,i}^u,
\mathcal K_{h,n,i}^\omega,
\mathcal K_{h,n,i}^m,
\mathcal K_{h,n,i}^\varphi
\bigr)^\intercal,
&\qquad
\widehat{\bs x}_{h,n,i}
&=
\bigl(
\widehat{\bs u}_{h,n,i},
\widehat{\bs\omega}_{h,n,i},
\widehat{\bs m}_{h,n,i},
\widehat{\varphi}_{h,n,i}
\bigr)^\intercal,
\\
\bs x_{h,n,i}
&=
\bigl(
\bs u_{h,n,i},
\bs\omega_{h,n,i},
\bs m_{h,n,i},
\varphi_{h,n,i}
\bigr)^\intercal,
&\qquad
\bs y_h
&=
\bigl(
\bs v_h,
\bs s_h,
\bs F_h,
\psi_h
\bigr)^\intercal.
\end{aligned}
\]
Equations~\eqref{eq:ho:FE-scheme-b} and
\eqref{eq:ho:FE-scheme-c} can then be written equivalently in the
following componentwise form.

\noindent
(1) Velocity and incompressibility equations.
\begin{subequations}\label{eq:NS-sol}
\begin{align}
\rho(\mathcal K_{h,n,i}^u,\bs v_h)
&+
\rho b(\widehat{\bs u}_{h,n,i},\bs u_{h,n,i},\bs v_h)
+
\eta(\nabla\bs u_{h,n,i},\nabla\bs v_h)
+
\zeta(\curl\bs u_{h,n,i},\curl\bs v_h)
\notag\\
&-
2\zeta(\bs\omega_{h,n,i},\curl\bs v_h)
-
(P_{h,n,i},\Div\bs v_h)
+
\frac{\mu_0}{2}
(\vartheta_{h,n,i},
 \widehat{\bs m}_{h,n,i}\cdot\bs v_h)
\notag\\
&+
\frac{\mu_0}{2}
(\nabla\varphi_{h,n,i}\Div\widehat{\bs m}_{h,n,i},
 \bs v_h)
-
\frac{\mu_0}{2}
(\bs v_h\times\widehat{\bs k}_{h,n,i},
 \nabla\varphi_{h,n,i})
\notag\\
&-
\frac{\mu_0}{2}
(\widehat{\bs m}_{h,n,i}\times\curl\bs v_h,
 \nabla\varphi_{h,n,i})
=
0,
\\
(\Div\bs u_{h,n,i},q_h)
&=
0.
\end{align}
\end{subequations}

\noindent
(2) Angular-velocity equation.
\begin{equation}\label{eq:angular-sol}
\begin{aligned}
\rho\kappa(\mathcal K_{h,n,i}^\omega,\bs s_h)
&+
\eta^\prime(\nabla\bs\omega_{h,n,i},\nabla\bs s_h)
+
(\eta^\prime+\lambda^\prime)
(\Div\bs\omega_{h,n,i},\Div\bs s_h)
+
4\zeta(\bs\omega_{h,n,i},\bs s_h)
\\
&+
\rho\kappa
b(\widehat{\bs u}_{h,n,i},\bs\omega_{h,n,i},\bs s_h)
-
\mu_0
(\widehat{\bs m}_{h,n,i}\times\nabla\varphi_{h,n,i},
 \bs s_h)
\\
&-
2\zeta(\curl\bs u_{h,n,i},\bs s_h)
=
0.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

\noindent
(3) Magnetization equation.
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mag-sol}
\begin{aligned}
(\mathcal K_{h,n,i}^m,\bs F_h)
&+
\frac{1}{\tau}(\bs m_{h,n,i},\bs F_h)
+
\sigma(\curl\bs k_{h,n,i},\bs F_h)
+
\sigma(\Div\bs m_{h,n,i},\Div\bs F_h)
\\
&-
c(\widehat{\bs u}_{h,n,i},\bs m_{h,n,i},\bs F_h)
-
\frac12
(\bs m_{h,n,i}\times\curl\widehat{\bs u}_{h,n,i},
 \bs F_h)
\\
&-
\frac12
(\widehat{\bs u}_{h,n,i}\times\bs k_{h,n,i},
 \bs F_h)
-
\frac12
(\curl\bs z_{h,n,i},\bs F_h)
\\
&-
(\widehat{\bs\omega}_{h,n,i}\times\bs m_{h,n,i},
 \bs F_h)
-
\frac{\chi_0}{\tau}
(\nabla\varphi_{h,n,i},\bs F_h)
=
0.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

\noindent
(4) Magnetostatic potential equation.
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mag-sta-sol}
\begin{aligned}
\mu_0(\mathcal K_{h,n,i}^\varphi,\nabla\psi_h)
&+
\frac{\mu_0(1+\chi_0)+\chi_0}{\tau}
(\nabla\varphi_{h,n,i},\nabla\psi_h)
+
\sigma\mu_0
(\nabla\vartheta_{h,n,i},\nabla\psi_h)
\\
&+
\mu_0
(\bs\omega_{h,n,i}\times\widehat{\bs m}_{h,n,i},
 \nabla\psi_h)
+
\frac{\chi_0}{\tau}
(\bs m_{h,n,i},\nabla\psi_h)
\\
&-
\frac{\mu_0}{2}
(\nabla\varpi_{h,n,i},\nabla\psi_h)
-
\frac{\mu_0}{2}
(\bs u_{h,n,i}\Div\widehat{\bs m}_{h,n,i},
 \nabla\psi_h)
\\
&+
\frac{\mu_0}{2}
(\widehat{\bs m}_{h,n,i}\times\curl\bs u_{h,n,i},
 \nabla\psi_h)
+
\frac{\mu_0}{2}
(\bs u_{h,n,i}\times\widehat{\bs k}_{h,n,i},
 \nabla\psi_h)
\\
&+
(\partial_t\bs H_e(t_{n,i}),\nabla\psi_h)
+
\frac{\mu_0+\chi_0}{\tau\mu_0}
(\bs H_e(t_{n,i}),\nabla\psi_h)
\\
&+
\sigma
(\nabla\Div\bs H_e(t_{n,i}),\nabla\psi_h)
=
0.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

\noindent
(5) Auxiliary-variable relations.
The auxiliary variables
\[
\widehat{\bs k}_{h,n,i},\bs k_{h,n,i},\bs z_{h,n,i}\in\bs U_h,
\qquad
\vartheta_{h,n,i},\varpi_{h,n,i}\in\Sigma_h
\]
are defined by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lag-sol}
\begin{aligned}
(\widehat{\bs k}_{h,n,i},\bs\theta_h)
&=
(\widehat{\bs m}_{h,n,i},\curl\bs\theta_h),
&&
\forall\,\bs\theta_h\in\bs U_h,
\\
(\bs k_{h,n,i},\bs\theta_h)
&=
(\bs m_{h,n,i},\curl\bs\theta_h),
&&
\forall\,\bs\theta_h\in\bs U_h,
\\
(\vartheta_{h,n,i},\varsigma_h)
&=
(\nabla\varphi_{h,n,i},\nabla\varsigma_h),
&&
\forall\,\varsigma_h\in\Sigma_h,
\\
(\bs z_{h,n,i},\bs\lambda_h)
&=
(\widehat{\bs u}_{h,n,i}\times\bs m_{h,n,i},
 \bs\lambda_h),
&&
\forall\,\bs\lambda_h\in\bs U_h,
\\
(\varpi_{h,n,i},\gamma_h)
&=
(\bs u_{h,n,i}\cdot\widehat{\bs m}_{h,n,i},
 \gamma_h),
&&
\forall\,\gamma_h\in\Sigma_h.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

%\subsection{Exact satisfaction of the curl constraint}

\begin{remark}
At each endpoint and each Runge--Kutta stage, define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:discrete-H}
 \bs H_h^n=\nabla\varphi_h^n\in\bs U_h,\qquad\chg{\bs H_{h,n,i}=\nabla\varphi_{h,n,i}\in\bs U_h.}
\end{equation}
It is easy to see that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:curl-free}
 \curl\bs H_h^n=0,\qquad\chg{\curl\bs H_{h,n,j}=0.}
\end{equation}
\end{remark}
%in the distributional sense and as a polynomial on each element. This statement does not require pointwise derivatives on element interfaces. A computed nonzero elementwise curl near machine precision reflects arithmetic or assembly effects, not a discretization error in the exact sequence.

%In contrast, the weak magnetostatic residual
%\begin{equation}\label{eq:magnetic-residual-h}
% \mathscr R_h^n(\psi_h)
% =-(\mu_0\nabla\varphi_h^n+\mu_0\bs m_h^n+\bs H_e(t_n),\nabla\psi_h)
%\end{equation}
%is not separately constrained by the evolution schemes. It is zero at $n=0$ by \eqref{eq:discrete-phi0}, but neither \eqref{eq:curl-free} nor energy stability implies $\mathscr R_h^n=0$ for later times. This residual should therefore be monitored when the method is used to approximate the original magnetostatic problem.

\subsection{An exact block elimination}\label{subsec:schur}
\chg{For a one-stage solve, group the coefficient vectors as}
\[
\begin{aligned}
X_1 &= (\bs u_{h,n,i},P_{h,n,i}),
&\qquad
X_2 &= \bs\omega_{h,n,i},
&\qquad
X_3 &= (\bs m_{h,n,i},\bs z_{h,n,i},\bs k_{h,n,i}),\\[1mm]
X_4 &= (\varphi_{h,n,i},\vartheta_{h,n,i}),
&\qquad
X_5 &= \varpi_{h,n,i}.
\end{aligned}
\]
%\begin{currentchange}
%For a multistage solve, use the same five physical groups and stack all
%$s$ stages within each $X_r$. To see why the block pattern is unchanged,
Eliminate the stage derivatives with $\mathsf S=\mathsf A^{-1}$,
%let $M_h$ be the matrix of $\DD$, $L_{n,i}$ the matrix of
%$\Aform_h+\JJ+\BB_h(\widehat{\bs x}_{h,n,i},\cdot,\cdot)$, and $C_h$ the
%pressure coupling matrix. In stage-major ordering the state--pressure
%system is
%\begin{equation}\label{eq:ho:global-matrix}
% \begin{pmatrix}
% \dt^{-1}(\mathsf S\otimes M_h)+\operatorname{diag}_i L_{n,i}
%                    &I_s\otimes C_h\\
% I_s\otimes C_h^{\mathsf T}&0
% \end{pmatrix}
% \begin{pmatrix}\mathbf x\\\mathbf p\end{pmatrix}
% =\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf r\\0\end{pmatrix},
%\end{equation}
%where
%$r_i=F_i+\dt^{-1}(\mathsf S\bs1)_iM_h\bs x_h^n$.
%The mass couplings connect different stages of the same physical variable.
%The spatial couplings and the auxiliary mass equations act within each
%stage. Therefore, after stacking by physical variable, the mass couplings
%enter the diagonal physical blocks $K_r$, and the same Schur formulas below
%apply to the enlarged blocks. No independent-stage solve is assumed.
%
after incorporating the known terms into the loads and fixing each stage
pressure mean, both the one-stage and multistage systems have the block pattern
%\end{currentchange}

\begin{equation}\label{eq:block-system}
 \begin{pmatrix}
 K_1&C_{12}&0&C_{14}&0\\
 C_{21}&K_2&0&C_{24}&0\\
 0&0&K_3&C_{34}&0\\
 C_{41}&C_{42}&C_{43}&K_4&C_{45}\\
 C_{51}&0&0&0&K_5
 \end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}X_1\\ X_2\\ X_3\\ X_4\\ X_5\end{pmatrix}
 =\begin{pmatrix}F_1\\F_2\\F_3\\F_4\\F_5\end{pmatrix}.
\end{equation}
To give a compact exact reduction, set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:schur-definitions}
\begin{aligned}
 H_{12}&=\begin{pmatrix}K_1&C_{12}\\C_{21}&K_2\end{pmatrix},&
 B_{12}&=\begin{pmatrix}C_{14}\\C_{24}\end{pmatrix},&
 F_{12}&=\begin{pmatrix}F_1\\F_2\end{pmatrix},\\
 E_1&=\begin{pmatrix}I&0\end{pmatrix},&
 G_{12}&=\begin{pmatrix}C_{41}&C_{42}\end{pmatrix}
              -C_{45}K_5^{-1}C_{51}E_1.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
%The inverse notation denotes a linear solve, not explicit matrix inversion. 
First solve the potential Schur system
\begin{equation}\label{eq:schur}
\begin{aligned}
 S_4X_4&=g_4,\\
 S_4&=K_4-G_{12}H_{12}^{-1}B_{12}-C_{43}K_3^{-1}C_{34},\\
 g_4&=F_4-C_{45}K_5^{-1}F_5-G_{12}H_{12}^{-1}F_{12}-C_{43}K_3^{-1}F_3.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Then recover the other blocks by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:schur-recovery}
 \begin{pmatrix}X_1\\ X_2\end{pmatrix}=H_{12}^{-1}(F_{12}-B_{12}X_4),\qquad
 X_3=K_3^{-1}(F_3-C_{34}X_4),\qquad
 X_5=K_5^{-1}(F_5-C_{51}X_1).
\end{equation}
%\begin{proposition}[Algebraic exactness]\label{prop:schur}
%\chg{With a fixed pressure gauge at each stage, the one-stage or stage-stacked blocks $H_{12}$, $K_3$, and $K_5$ are invertible.} Equations~\eqref{eq:schur}--\eqref{eq:schur-recovery} are equivalent to \eqref{eq:block-system}; hence $S_4$ is invertible for each uniquely solvable \chg{one-stage or multistage system}.
%\end{proposition}
%\begin{proof}
%%\begin{currentchange}
%For one stage, the velocity--spin state block is coercive and its pressure
%coupling is inf-sup stable. Eliminating the auxiliaries in $K_3$ leaves a
%positive mass-plus-diffusion form and skew transport and rotation terms.
%For several stages, multiply the $i$th variational row by $b_i$ and use
%\eqref{eq:ho:inverse-positivity}. The mass contribution is nonnegative, and
%the summed velocity--spin or magnetization dissipation is coercive. The
%product-space argument and the stagewise inf-sup condition then prove the
%same invertibility statements. The block $K_5$ is a scalar mass matrix or
%a block diagonal collection of such matrices.
%%\end{currentchange}
%Solve rows one and two of \eqref{eq:block-system} for $(X_1,X_2)$, row three for $X_3$, and row five for $X_5$. In row four, eliminating $X_5$ replaces $(C_{41},C_{42})$ by $G_{12}$ and replaces $F_4$ by $F_4-C_{45}K_5^{-1}F_5$. Substitution of the remaining solved blocks gives exactly \eqref{eq:schur}. Reversing these substitutions proves equivalence. Unique solvability of the full \chg{one-stage or multistage system} then implies invertibility of its Schur complement.
%\end{proof}
%
%\chg{For Runge--Kutta, the reduced potential unknown contains all stage potentials.} The reduction does not remove the coupling represented by $S_4$. Forming $S_4$ explicitly can cause fill-in. A practical implementation may instead apply its action through block solves and use a suitable iterative method, but mesh-independent iteration counts or a computational speedup require separate analysis and timing experiments. For the nonlinear starting step, an iterative solver must also enforce the nonlinear residual to an accuracy consistent with the desired discretization error; the linear energy argument does not prove convergence of that iteration.


\section{Numerical experiments}
\label{sec:numerics}

In this section, we test convergence and energy decay for the
first- through fourth-order schemes, and use the second-order scheme
for two magnetically driven channel-flow examples. All computations
are performed with FEniCSx/DOLFINx~0.10.0~\cite{Barattafenics},
PETSc, and MUMPS.

For the first-order scheme \eqref{eq:first-order-eq}, we use the
MINI element for the velocity--pressure pair $(\bs u,\tilde p)$,
$RT_0$ for $\bs m$, $NE_0$ for $\bs H$, $\bs k$, and $\bs z$,
continuous piecewise $\mathbb P_1$ vector elements for $\bs\omega$,
and continuous piecewise $\mathbb P_1$ scalar elements for
$\varphi$, $\vartheta$, and $\varpi$ in
\eqref{eq:NS-sol}--\eqref{eq:lag-sol}. For the second-, third-,
and fourth-order schemes
\eqref{eq:second-order-eq},
\eqref{eq:ho:order-three-scheme}, and
\eqref{eq:ho:Gauss-four-scheme}, respectively, we use the
Taylor--Hood pair $\mathbb P_k\times\mathbb P_{k-1}$ for
$(\bs u,\tilde p)$, $RT_{k-1}$ for $\bs m$, and $NE_{k-1}$ for
$\bs H$, $\bs k$, and $\bs z$, where $k=2,3,4$. The variables
$\bs\omega$ and $\varphi$, $\vartheta$, and $\varpi$ are
approximated by continuous piecewise $\mathbb P_k$ vector and scalar
elements, respectively.

%In this section, we first present convergence tests and unforced energy-dissipation experiments for the first- through fourth-order time discretizations. We then consider two magnetically driven channel-flow problems to demonstrate the performance of the second-order scheme. All computations are carried out using FEniCSx/DOLFINx~0.10.0~\cite{Barattafenics}, together with PETSc and MUMPS.
%
%In all the following examples, for the first-order temporal scheme \eqref{eq:first-order-eq}, we use the MINI finite element pair to discretize the velocity--pressure variables $\bs u$ and $\tilde p$, the $RT_{0}$ element to discretize $\bs m$, the $NE_{0}$ element to discretize $\bs H$, $\bs k$, and $\bs z$, a continuous piecewise $\mathbb P_{1}$ vector finite element space to discretize $\bs\omega$, and a continuous piecewise $\mathbb P_{1}$ finite element space to discretize $\varphi$, $\vartheta$, and $\varpi$ in the equivalent formulation \eqref{eq:NS-sol}--\eqref{eq:lag-sol} of \eqref{eq:ho:FE-scheme}. For the $k$th-order ($k=2,3,4$) temporal schemes \eqref{eq:second-order-eq}, \eqref{eq:ho:order-three-scheme}, and \eqref{eq:ho:Gauss-four-scheme}, we use the Taylor--Hood finite element pair $\mathbb P_k\times\mathbb P_{k-1}$ to discretize $\bs u$ and $\tilde p$, the $RT_{k-1}$ element to discretize $\bs m$, the $NE_{k-1}$ element to discretize $\bs H$, $\bs k$, and $\bs z$, a continuous piecewise $\mathbb P_k$ vector finite element space to discretize $\bs\omega$, and a continuous piecewise $\mathbb P_k$ finite element space to discretize $\varphi$, $\vartheta$, and $\varpi$.

\subsection{Convergence test}
\label{subsec:convergencetest}

We compare the first- through fourth-order schemes using one common
continuous manufactured solution on $\Omega=(0,1)^3$, with $T=2$ and all
physical parameters $\rho,\kappa,\eta,\zeta,\mu_0,\sigma,
\eta',\lambda',\tau,\chi_0$ equal to one. Let
\[
 a(t)=e^{t/2},\qquad B(x,y,z)=x(1-x)y(1-y)z(1-z).
\]
The prescribed fields are
\begin{align*}
 \bs u&=\frac{a(t)}{C_u}\nabla B\times(1,2,3)^\intercal,\\
 \bs\omega&=\frac{a(t)}{C_\omega}
 (1+x^5+yz^4,\ 2+y^5+xz^4,\ -1+z^5+xy^4)^\intercal,\\
 \bs m&=\frac{a(t)}{C_m}
 (1+x^5+y^2z^3,\ 2+y^5+z^2x^3,\ 3+z^5+x^2y^3)^\intercal,\\
 \varphi&=\frac{a(t)}{C_H}
 (1+x^5+2y^5+3z^5+x^2y^2z),\\
 \pp&=\frac{a(t)}{C_p}
 (x^4+2y^4-3z^4+xy^2z-1/12),\qquad
 \bs H=\nabla\varphi,\qquad \bs k=\curl\bs m.
\end{align*}
Here $\pp$ is the zero-mean shifted pressure in
\eqref{eq:shifted-pressure}; a corresponding physical pressure is
$p=\pp+\mu_0\bs m\cdot\bs H/2$. The normalization constants, computed
from continuous norms at $t=0$ and fixed for all meshes and orders, are
\[
\begin{aligned}
 C_u&=0.5849976258261415,& C_\omega&=4.301498111459077,\\
 C_m&=5.636941389185116,& C_H&=6.437390775772433,\\
 C_p&=1.0210677573400138.&&
\end{aligned}
\]
The remaining auxiliary fields are
$\bs z=\bs u\times\bs m$, $\vartheta=-\Div\bs H$, and
$\varpi=\bs u\cdot\bs m$.

For this manufactured test, continuous body sources $\bs f$, $\bs g$,
and $\bs r$ are added to the momentum, angular-momentum, and
magnetization equations, respectively, and are obtained by substitution
of the prescribed fields into the strong equations. We take
\[
 \bs H_e=-\mu_0(\bs m+\bs H),\qquad
 \bs G_e=-\partial_t\bs H_e-\beta\bs H_e
           +\sigma\nabla\Div\bs H_e.
\]
The corresponding load in the potential equation is formed consistently
from $\bs G_e-\mu_0\bs r$. The sources and boundary traces are independent
of the mesh, the scheme order, the time step, and the computed solution.
The particular choice of $\bs H_e$ is part of this manufactured test.

The prescribed traces are generally nonhomogeneous. They are imposed
using boundary-data lifts for the four dynamic fields
$(\bs u,\bs\omega,\bs m,\varphi)$, including the known lift derivatives
and endpoint corrections; the nonlinear predictors retain the numerical
physical-field history. Initially, the velocity is projected with its
discrete divergence constraint, the angular velocity uses an $L^2$
projection, and the magnetization uses an $L^2$ projection constrained by
the prescribed normal trace and the moments of its divergence in the
corresponding discontinuous polynomial space. The scalar potential is
initialized by the compatible Maxwell--Poisson constraint with the
prescribed trace. The auxiliary endpoint fields $\bs k$ and $\vartheta$
are recovered through their weak mass relations. These choices specify
the nonhomogeneous extension used in this experiment; the homogeneous
boundary assumptions of the preceding analysis are not silently imposed
on this manufactured solution. The energy-decay experiment below has its
own homogeneous-boundary initial data.

We use $K\times K\times K$ uniform tetrahedral meshes and
$\dt=1/(2K)$. The first- and second-order schemes use
$K=2,4,8,16,32$, the third-order scheme uses $K=2,4,8,16$, and the
fourth-order scheme uses $K=2,4,8$. Thus the comparison contains seventeen
completed cases. The finite element spaces are those specified above,
and the starting steps use the prescribed fixed number of linear
corrections. Space and time are refined together in this test.

For $v\in\{\bs u,\bs\omega,\bs m,\bs H,\bs k,\varphi\}$, let
$X_v$ denote, respectively, $H^1$, $H^1$, $H(\Div)$, $H(\curl)$,
$L^2$, and $H^1$. We report
\[
 E_{v,\mathrm{abs}}=\|v_h^N-v(T)\|_{X_v},\qquad
 E_{v,\mathrm{rel}}=
 \frac{E_{v,\mathrm{abs}}}{\|v(T)\|_{X_v}}.
\]
The $H^1$, $H(\Div)$, and $H(\curl)$ quantities are full norms, not
seminorms. Pressure is measured at the actual Runge--Kutta stages of the
last time step. With $t_{N-1,i}=T-\dt+c_i\dt$ and
\mbox{$v^\circ=v-|\Omega|^{-1}\int_\Omega v\dd x$}, its errors are
\begin{align*}
 E_{\pp,\mathrm{abs}}
 &=\left(\sum_{i=1}^s b_i
  \|P_{h,N-1,i}^{\circ}-\pp(t_{N-1,i})^{\circ}\|_{L^2}^2\right)^{1/2},\\
 R_{\pp}
 &=\left(\sum_{i=1}^s b_i
  \|\pp(t_{N-1,i})^{\circ}\|_{L^2}^2\right)^{1/2},\qquad
 E_{\pp,\mathrm{rel}}=E_{\pp,\mathrm{abs}}/R_{\pp}.
\end{align*}
The table heading $\pp_{\rm stg}$ denotes this stage-weighted quantity.
It coincides with an endpoint pressure error for backward Euler, whose
only stage has $c_1=1$. For the Gauss schemes it is neither an endpoint
pressure error at $T$ nor a pressure norm accumulated over all time steps.
For either error measure, the observed rate between consecutive meshes
is
\[
 \operatorname{Rate}(K_1,K_2)=
 \frac{\log(E(K_1)/E(K_2))}{\log(K_2/K_1)}.
\]
Rates are evaluated from the unrounded errors. The first row of each
table has no preceding mesh and is marked by a dash. The quantity
\[
 C_{\rm curl}=\max_{x\in\mathcal Q_h}\max_{j=1,2,3}
                   |(\curl\bs H_h(x))_j|
\]
is an absolute sampled curl diagnostic on the retained elementwise
evaluation points $\mathcal Q_h$, not a separately proved continuous
$L^\infty$ norm; no convergence rate is assigned to it.

\Cref{tab:first-order-errors,tab:second-order-errors,tab:third-order-errors,tab:fourth-order-errors} give all relative errors
and rates. The corresponding absolute errors and their rates are listed
in \cref{app:absolute-errors}. In particular, the pressure reference norm
$R_{\pp}$ varies with the stage times, so its absolute and relative
rates need not be identical.

On the finest adjacent meshes, the six nonpressure norms exhibit rates
close to the nominal first, second, third, and fourth orders. Pressure
has the measured finest-segment relative rates $1.7576$, $2.0202$,
$3.0754$, and $4.5270$, respectively. For the fourth-order scheme, the
absolute pressure rate is $4.5048$; the difference of about $0.0222$
comes from the changing stage reference norm. The first-order reference
pressure norm is evaluated at $T$ on every mesh, so its absolute and
relative rates agree up to roundoff.

These pressure values are finite-grid observations. Contributions from
space, time, initialization, and the nonhomogeneous boundary treatment
have not been separated by this joint refinement. In particular,
\cref{thm:ho:convergence} estimates the endpoint and accumulated stage
errors of the state variables, not this last-step pressure norm. The
tables therefore do not establish an independent temporal or spatial
order, or a general pressure superconvergence result. The sampled curl
values are reported as finite-precision diagnostics of the gradient
representation. The third-order $K=8$ endpoint was independently
remeasured from archived terminal fields after a monitoring failure;
no time step was repeated for that endpoint measurement.

\clearpage
\subsection{Energy dissipation test}

This subsection is to investigate the energy decaying phenomenon of the $k$th-order ($k=1,2,3,4$) temporal schemes \eqref{eq:first-order-eq}, \eqref{eq:second-order-eq}, \eqref{eq:ho:order-three-scheme}, and \eqref{eq:ho:Gauss-four-scheme}. The finite element spaces are choose same as in \cref{subsec:convergencetest}. We consider the FHD model \eqref{eq:FHD} with external magnetic field $\bs H_{e} = 0$ and homogeneous boundary conditions. The initial value functions are specified below.
The symbols in these initial data are defined independently of the
manufactured solution in \cref{subsec:convergencetest}. Specifically,
\[
\begin{aligned}
 q_{\rm E}(s)&=64s^3(1-s)^3,\qquad
 \Phi_{\rm E}(x,y,z)=q_{\rm E}(x)q_{\rm E}(y)q_{\rm E}(z),\\
 S_{\rm E}(x,y,z)&=\sin^2(\pi x)\sin^2(\pi y)\sin^2(\pi z),\\
 \bs a_{\rm E}&=(1,2,3)^\intercal,\quad
 \bs b_{\rm E}=(1,-1,1/2)^\intercal,\quad
 \bs c_{\rm E}=(1,2,-1)^\intercal,\\
 C_{u,\rm E}&=363.275188821,\quad
 C_{\omega,\rm E}=20.6500992827,\quad
 C_{m,\rm E}=\frac{\sqrt{81+108\pi^2}}{16}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Thus the original initial fields used for the energy curves remain
\[
\bs u_0=C_{u,\rm E}^{-1}\nabla\Phi_{\rm E}\times\bs a_{\rm E},\qquad
\bs\omega_0=C_{\omega,\rm E}^{-1}\Phi_{\rm E}\bs b_{\rm E},\qquad
\bs m_0=C_{m,\rm E}^{-1}S_{\rm E}\bs c_{\rm E}.
\]
All the physical parameters remain one. We show in \cref{fig:energy-comparison} the discrete energy curve of $E_{h}$ at the spatial mesh with $K=8$ and $\dt = 1/16,~1/32,~1/64$.

    \centering

        \centering
        \includegraphics[width=\linewidth]
        {figures/first_order_energy.pdf}
        \caption{First-order scheme.}
        \label{fig:first-order-energy}
    \end{subfigure}
    \hfill
    \begin{subfigure}[t]{0.48\textwidth}
        \centering
        \includegraphics[width=\linewidth]
        {figures/second_order_energy.pdf}
        \caption{Second-order scheme.}
        \label{fig:second-order-energy}
    \end{subfigure}
    \caption{Discrete energy evolution for the first- and second-order schemes.}
    \label{fig:energy-comparison}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Magnetically forced flows
}
\label{subsec:straight-channel}

In this subsection, we apply the second-order scheme
\eqref{eq:second-order-eq} to a three-dimensional ferrofluid transport
problem with prescribed inflow. 
%We use the Taylor--Hood pair
%\(\mathbb P_2\times\mathbb P_1\) for \(\bs u\) and \(\tilde p\),
%the \(RT_1\) element for \(\bs m\), and the \(NE_1\) element for
%\(\bs H\), \(\bs k\), and \(\bs z\). The angular velocity
%\(\bs\omega\) is approximated by continuous piecewise quadratic vector
%elements, while \(\varphi\), \(\vartheta\), and \(\varpi\) are
%approximated by continuous piecewise quadratic scalar elements.
%
These examples are inspired by the channel-pumping tests in
Dong et al.~\cite{DongHuangHuangYang2026}, but it does not reproduce
their parameter settings. Instead, we retain the magnetic diffusion,
edge and face finite element spaces, and midpoint--extrapolation scheme
used in the present work. All quantities are
dimensionless.


%This subsection, we apply the second-order scheme \eqref{eq:second-order-eq}, with Taylor--Hood finite element pair $\mathbb P_2\times\mathbb P_{1}$ to discretize $\bs u$ and $\tilde p$, the $RT_{1}$ element to discretize $\bs m$, the $NE_{1}$ element to discretize $\bs H$, $\bs k$, and $\bs z$, a continuous piecewise $\mathbb P_2$ vector finite element space to discretize $\bs\omega$, and a continuous piecewise $\mathbb P_2$ finite element space to discretize $\varphi$, $\vartheta$, and $\varpi$,
%to three-dimensional ferrofluid transport with prescribed inflow.
%The configuration is motivated by the channel-pumping examples of
%Dong et al.~\cite{DongHuangHuangYang2026}, while retaining the magnetic
%diffusion, compatible finite element spaces, and midpoint/extrapolation
%scheme of the present work. The parameters and driving waveform below
%define our computational example; they are not a reproduction of all
%the parameters in that reference. All quantities are dimensionless.

\begin{example}[Magnetically forced flow in a straight channel]\label{ex:str-cha}
We consider the straight channel
\(\Omega=(0,1)\times(0,0.2)\times(0,0.2)\), with inlet
\(\Gamma_i=\{x=0\}\), outlet \(\Gamma_o=\{x=1\}\), and solid walls
\(\Gamma_w=\partial\Omega\setminus(\Gamma_i\cup\Gamma_o)\). The material
parameters are
\[
\rho=\mu_0=\chi_0=1,\qquad
\kappa=\zeta=0.01,\qquad
\eta=0.02,\qquad
\eta^\prime=\lambda^\prime=0.001,\qquad
\tau=0.1,\qquad
\sigma=0.005,
\]
and all body sources and initial values are set to zero. The inlet velocity is
\[
\bs u_D
=
\bigl(4R(t/0.4)Y(1-Y)Z(1-Z),0,0\bigr)^\intercal,
\qquad
Y=\frac{y}{0.2},\quad Z=\frac{z}{0.2},
\]
where
\[
R(s)=
\begin{cases}
0,&s\le0,\\
10s^3-15s^4+6s^5,&0<s<1,\\
1,&s\ge1.
\end{cases}
\]
The magnetic field is generated by eight dipoles located outside the channel at
\(\bs x_i^-=(0.2i,-0.15,0.1)\) and
\(\bs x_i^+=(0.2i,0.35,0.1)\), \(i=1,\ldots,4\), with directions
\(\bs d_i^-=(0,1,0)^\intercal\) and
\(\bs d_i^+=(0,-1,0)^\intercal\). Their potential and time-dependent strengths
are
%\[
%\begin{aligned}
%\Phi_a(\bs x,t)
%& =
%\sum_{i=1}^4 a_i(t)
%\sum_{\epsilon\in\{-,+\}}
%\frac{\bs d_i^\epsilon\cdot(\bs x_i^\epsilon-\bs x)}
%     {|\bs x_i^\epsilon-\bs x|^3}, \\
%a_i(t)
%& =
%3\times10^{-4}A
%R\!\left(\frac{t-0.2}{0.4}\right)
%\left[
%0.7+0.3\cos\!\left(2\pi\left(t-\frac{0.2i}{0.8}\right)\right)
%\right],
%\end{aligned}
%\]
%
\begin{align}
 \Phi_a(\bs x,t)
 &=\sum_{i=1}^4 a_i(t)
   \sum_{\epsilon\in\{-,+\}}
   \frac{\bs d_i^{\epsilon}\cdot(\bs x_i^{\epsilon}-\bs x)}
   {|\bs x_i^{\epsilon}-\bs x|^3},
 &\bs h_a&=\nabla\Phi_a,\label{eq:channel-dipoles}\\
 a_i(t)
 &=C_a A R\!\left(\frac{t-0.2}{0.4}\right)
 \left[0.7+0.3\cos\!\left(2\pi\left(t-\frac{0.2i}{0.8}\right)\right)\right],
 &C_a&=3\times10^{-4},\label{eq:channel-pulse}
\end{align}
with \(\bs H_e=-\mu_0\bs h_a\). We take
\(A=1\) for the magnetic case and \(A=0\) for the zero-field control. Since the
dipoles lie outside \(\Omega\), \(\Phi_a\) is harmonic in the fluid region,
and the magnetic-potential load is
\[
(\bs G_e,\nabla\psi_h),
\qquad
\bs G_e
=
\mu_0\partial_t\bs h_a
+
\frac{\mu_0+\chi_0}{\tau}\bs h_a.
\]
We impose \(\bs u=\bs0\) on \(\Gamma_w\), the gradient-traction condition
\[
\bigl((\eta+\zeta)\nabla\bs u-pI\bigr)\bs n=\bs0
\qquad\text{on }\Gamma_o,
\]
and
\[
\bs\omega=\bs0,\qquad
\bs m\cdot\bs n=0,\qquad
\bs k\times\bs n=\bs0,\qquad
(\bs H+\bs m)\cdot\bs n=\bs h_a\cdot\bs n
\qquad\text{on }\partial\Omega,
\]
together with \(\int_{\Omega}\varphi_h\,\mathrm dx=0\). The prescribed normal
magnetic load is included in the weak definition of \(\vartheta_h\). 


We set \(\Delta t=0.005\) and \(T=2\).
The magnetic simulations were performed on meshes with \(K=4,8,12\),
while the zero-field control used \(K=12\). As shown in
\Cref{fig:channel-u,fig:channel-m,fig:channel-omega}, the velocity at
\(t=2\) remains mainly aligned with the channel, whereas the
magnetization follows the nonuniform dipole field and the particle
angular velocity is concentrated in regions of strong shear; in
particular, it is different from the fluid vorticity
\(\curl\bs u_h\). The relative \(L^2\) differences between the
\(K=8\) and \(K=12\) solutions are \(0.13524\%\), \(3.29718\%\),
\(0.71893\%\), and \(3.14611\%\) for \(\bs u\), \(\bs m\),
\(\bs\omega\), and \(\bs H\), respectively. These values measure
differences between two successive meshes and are not errors relative
to an exact solution. In the zero-field control, both the magnetization
and magnetic field vanish, while the differences from the magnetic
case are \(0.00725\%\) in velocity and \(0.15803\%\) in angular
velocity. The results show the coupled response to the applied magnetic
field, but they do not by themselves establish mesh-converged magnetic
pumping or an increase in the prescribed through-flow.

\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/applications/channel_u.pdf}
\caption{Straight-channel flow at $t=2$ with $K=12$: velocity vectors
(left) and the velocity magnitude on $z=0.1$ (right).}
\label{fig:channel-u}
\end{figure}
\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/applications/channel_m.pdf}
\caption{Straight-channel flow at $t=2$ with $K=12$: magnetization
vectors (left) and the magnetization magnitude on $z=0.1$ (right).}
\label{fig:channel-m}
\end{figure}
\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/applications/channel_omega.pdf}
\caption{Straight-channel flow at $t=2$ with $K=12$: particle angular
velocity vectors (left) and their magnitude on $z=0.1$ (right).
The arrows display instantaneous vectors, not particle trajectories.}
\label{fig:channel-omega}
\end{figure}

\end{example}

\begin{example}[Magnetically forced flow over a backward-facing step]
This example, we consider the backward-facing-step channel
\begin{equation}\label{eq:step-domain}
 \Omega
 =
 (0,1)\times(0,0.2)\times(0,0.2)
 \setminus
 \bigl([0,0.2]\times[0,0.2]\times[0,0.1]\bigr),
\end{equation}
which is motivated by the three-dimensional configuration in
\cite{DongHuangHuangYang2026}. Unless stated otherwise, the material
parameters, zero initial data, body forces, finite element spaces,
boundary conditions, time step, final time, quadrature rule, and solver
tolerances are the same as those used in
\Cref{ex:str-cha}. The inlet is the upper half of the face
\(x=0\), and the prescribed velocity is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:step-inlet}
 \bs u_D
 =
 \bigl(
 4R(t/0.4)Y(1-Y)Z_s(1-Z_s),0,0
 \bigr)^\intercal,
 \qquad
 Y=\frac{y}{0.2},
 \qquad
 Z_s=\frac{z-0.1}{0.1},
\end{equation}
where \(R\) is defined in \Cref{ex:str-cha}. The same magnetic
potential and time-dependent waveform as in
\eqref{eq:channel-dipoles}--\eqref{eq:channel-pulse} are used, but the
dipoles are placed at
\[
 \bs x_i^-=(0.2i,-0.2,0.1),
 \qquad
 \bs x_i^+=(0.2i,0.4,0.1),
 \qquad i=1,\ldots,4,
\]
with
\[
 \bs d_i^-=(0,1,0)^\intercal,
 \qquad
 \bs d_i^+=(0,-1,0)^\intercal,
 \qquad
 C_a=7\times10^{-4}.
\]
A conforming tetrahedral mesh is obtained from a
\(5K\times K\times K\) background partition after removing the solid
cells. To refine the region near the step, the axial coordinate is
mapped according to
\[
\begin{array}{c|ccccc}
 \xi&0&0.1&0.2&0.6&1\\
 \hline
 x&0&0.15&0.2&0.4&1
\end{array}.
\]
The resulting mesh contains \(27K^3\) tetrahedra. The results below use
\(K=4\) and show the solution at the stored time level \(t=0.715\).
As illustrated in
\Cref{fig:step-u,fig:step-m,fig:step-omega}, the velocity expands into
the larger downstream section, the magnetization follows the
nonuniform applied field, and the particle angular velocity is strongest
near the step and the channel walls. The maximum relative algebraic
residual over the simulation is \(2.03\times10^{-10}\), and the largest
relative inlet--outlet flux imbalance is \(1.12\times10^{-14}\). A
comparison with the smaller time step \(\Delta t=0.0025\) gives endpoint
relative \(L^2\) differences below \(2.9\times10^{-5}\) for
\(\bs u\), \(\bs m\), \(\bs\omega\), and \(\bs H\). These results
confirm the accuracy of the algebraic solver and show low sensitivity
to the chosen time step. However, the \(K=4\) results are intended only
as a qualitative illustration; mesh-independent flow quantities are
not claimed. The nonconvex geometry and open boundaries also lie outside
the homogeneous convex-domain setting of the analysis.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]
{figures/applications/step_u.pdf}
\caption{Backward-facing-step flow at \(t=0.715\) with \(K=4\): velocity vectors (left) and velocity magnitude on \(y=0.1\)
(right). The unfilled upstream region is the solid step.}
\label{fig:step-u}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]
{figures/applications/step_m.pdf}
\caption{Backward-facing-step flow at \(t=0.715\) with \(K=4\): magnetization vectors (left) and magnetization magnitude on
\(y=0.1\) (right).}
\label{fig:step-m}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]
{figures/applications/step_omega.pdf}
\caption{Backward-facing-step flow at \(t=0.715\) with \(K=4\): particle angular-velocity vectors (left) and their magnitude on
\(y=0.1\) (right).}
\label{fig:step-omega}
\end{figure}
\end{example}

\section{Absolute errors for the common manufactured-solution test}
\label{app:absolute-errors}

The following tables give the absolute errors for exactly the same
seventeen cases as \cref{subsec:convergencetest}, with $T=2$ and
$\dt=1/(2K)$. The six nonpressure fields use the terminal full norms;
$\pp_{\rm stg}$ uses the last-step stage-weighted $L^2$ norm defined
there. The rates below are computed from the unrounded absolute errors,
and hence may differ from the relative pressure rates. These tables
reuse the same accepted numerical outputs; no additional computation
is involved.

\section*{Declaration of Generative AI and AI-Assisted Technologies}

The initial draft of this manuscript, including the mathematical model,
the abstract formulation, and the main mathematical derivations, was
written and developed independently by the authors. During the revision
process, generative AI was used only to assist with improving the English
expression and the logical organization of the presentation, with the aim
of making the manuscript clearer and easier to read.

Generative AI was also used as an auxiliary tool in the construction of
the higher-order time discretizations. In particular, within the abstract
framework developed by the authors, it assisted in identifying candidate
Runge--Kutta coefficients for the third- and fourth-order methods that
satisfy the required stage-order, quadrature, and energy-stability
conditions. All AI-assisted suggestions, including the coefficients,
formulas, and related arguments, were independently checked and verified
by the authors. The authors take full responsibility for the mathematical
correctness, originality, and final content of the manuscript.
